Halo Infinite Adds 10 New Maps In Surprise November 2026 Update

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

They're part of the "Halo Fest Showcase" playlist

Who would have thought Halo Infinite would still be getting updates in November 2026? That's exactly what's happened this week though, and it's getting us tempted to install the multiplayer and play some games all over again.

The 10 new maps that have been added to the game this week are part of a Halo Fest Forge Contest that took place earlier this year, and they've specifically been added to a "Halo Fest Showcase" playlist.

You can see all the maps in the video up above, the list of which is as follows:

All of these are available across anything from 2-4 modes depending on the map, with those modes including CTF: Arena, King of the Hill: Arena, Oddball: Arena and Slayer: Arena.

There's nothing you need to do in terms of downloading anything on your Xbox Series X, Series S, Xbox One or PC as far as we're aware - they've all just been added in the background and are ready to play right now!

We'll leave you with a glimpse at a few of the maps we're most intrigued by down below.

Worst of weather can’t blow Eastbourne Borough’s league campaign off course

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Eastbourne Borough claimed three valuable Isthmian Premier points on Tuesday night with a 1-0 victory over lowly Stanway Rovers – but for manager Steve King, it was all about the elements as well as the opposition.

The Sports extended their incredible current run to 14 victories in 15 starts – just the 3-0 FA Cup defeat at Salisbury blotching the record – as they swept aside second-bottom Stanway Rovers at the Connect Management Stadium.

Did you know with an ad-lite subscription to SussexWorld, you get 70% fewer ads while viewing the news that matters to you.

Skipper Josh Rees notched the crucial goal just before half-time, and Borough spurned a whole clutch of chances to make the game safe against gritty opposition, in front of a huddled crowd of 619 – only a little over one-third of Saturday's attendance against Ramsgate.

The victory nudges the Sports up to third in the table, ahead of a potentially difficult trip to Dartford on Saturday.

Borough are, frankly, the team to beat right now, as this compelling campaign moves towards the winter. But for the players, supporters and the gaffer himself, Tuesday night was no feast of football, just a test of endurance.

"Shocking! Not my team, nor even the opposition, but the weather! If you don't know Eastbourne, you don't know our weather. It was horrendous - the wind especially - it's the worst I've ever seen it - and I live in this town!" said King.

"It was appalling and it ruined the game - no flow or pattern. It was just a match that you wanted to be finished, as soon as the referee blew his first whistle.

"But ultimately you just go about your business. Sometimes if you can 'win horribly' like that, it is actually a good sign. We can't always play great football and win. Long throws, headers off the line, you just do what you have to.

"But we did miss five unbelievable chances, and I mean five, to make the game safe. Their keeper has made three or four brilliant saves - but we should be putting those chances to bed.

"Poor as the game was, poor as the conditions were, we've had chances and we should have taken and made the game safe. So I was disappointed from that point of view, but… cross it off, game done. You can only get three points for a win, whatever the quality or the conditions or the opposition."

Is King: the new Michael Fish? Readers of a certain age will just still remember the infamous forecast in 1987 from the BBC weatherman - the night before a hurricane swept across southern England. Michael actually lived in Eastbourne: perhaps he should have known better.

There was no football stadium at Priory Lane back then, but the flat Langney landscape has always been open to all the elements. Never mind, you hardy Borough fans: not even the worst that the Channel throws at us can blow King's Sports off course.

Abdul El-Sayed defeats Mike Rogers despite Haley Stevens drama

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Democrat Abdul El-Sayed is projected to defeat Republican Mike RogersÂ for Michiganâs open U.S. Senate seat, a closely watched race that capped off a massive night for progressives.Â

The Associated Press called the race shortly after 2 a.m. Wednesday morning with El-Sayed leading by about seven points.Â

The candidates were competing to replace retiring Democratic Sen. Gary Peters, whose departure created one of the most consequential open-seat contests of the midterm elections. El-Sayed, a former Detroit health director, campaigned on expanding access to health care and lowering costs, while Rogers, a former congressman and House Intelligence Committee chairman, emphasized manufacturing jobs, tax cuts and his alignment with President Donald Trump.

The race took an unexpected turn just two days before Election Day when Democratic Rep. Haley Stevens, who narrowly lost the partyâs August primary to El-Sayed, endorsed Rogers during an appearance at a Detroit church.

The endorsement stunned Michigan Democrats, prompting condemnation from party leaders and resignations among Stevensâ staff. Stevens cited disagreements with El-Sayed over Israel and his campaignâs direction, while Democrats accused her of undermining their nominee in a critical Senate contest.

El-Sayed sought to move past the controversy, urging supporters to focus on voting. His campaign reported raising $2.4 million on Sunday, while Michigan Democratic officials said the endorsement had prompted an increase in volunteers. Rogers, meanwhile, highlighted Stevensâ support in the campaignâs final days.

More than 2.3 million Michigan voters had already cast ballots by Monday, according to The Associated Press, raising questions about how much the late-breaking controversy could influence the outcome.

Why a pink potato-like robot is wheeling around Philadelphia

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 4, 2026 2:17 PM EST

It was a showdown worthy of a classic Western, except the things rolling down the street weren’t tumbleweeds. They were robots.

On one side of an Old City sidewalk in Philadelphia, an Uber Eats robot trundled along on its daily route. Suddenly, something blocked its path, and it was met face-to-interface with another, new robot. This new bot on the block was wildly different from its utilitarian, food-conveying counterpart: Not only did it have blond hair, blue eyes, and a perpetually lolling tongue, but it also had a name. Meet Orifice the Awebot.

Meg Saligman, founder of the Ministry of Awe, an immersive art installation located in a 19th-century Old City bank building, had raced ahead to intercept the bot so Orifice could meet it. According to The Philadelphia Inquirer, Orifice’s creator, Madison Polidoro, then joked that the two robots were “gonna fight!”

The Uber Eats robot chose a pacifist approach, steering around its would-be adversary without incident.

But Orifice wasn’t created to instigate a territorial dispute between robots. She was made to promote peace between robots and humans, which is no small feat in the City of Brotherly Love. Here, delivery bots aren’t brothers. They’re the “other,” even when they’re bringing you a cheesesteak with Whiz.

Since Uber Eats delivery bots made their Philadelphia debut in March, the city has not exactly rolled out the red carpet. Within weeks of the robots’ launch, Philadelphians displayed “the whole spectrum” of reactions, according to Dr. Philip Dames, an associate professor of mechanical engineering at Temple University in Philly.  Most people barely gave them a second glance, but on the extreme end, one robot was kicked over at around midnight, and one was sat on and graffitied with the words, “DESTROY ME PLZ.”

Orifice was designed to be a more warmhearted, interactive answer to the joyless functionality of delivery bots. But why are some people compelled to harass delivery bots in the first place? And can a pink potato with a tongue change that?

The Ministry commissioned Polidoro, a Philadelphia-area fine artist and fabricator known as 10 Ton Fetus for her surreal and psychological sculptures, to create a mobile sculpture.

After talking it over with her colleagues, she decided to build her own robot, Orifice, as a foil to the Uber Eats bots.

“Having run into them, and being horrified by it, I was excited at the prospect of making their archnemesis, a hero of sorts!” she says. “Something perfectly opposite of what they [delivery robots] are.”

Polidoro knew that to properly offset the slick, appliance-like look of the Uber Eats bots, she would need to give Orifice plenty of human flourishes.

“She had to be cute, friendly, and human,” Polidoro says. “I carefully considered approachability and anchoring points for human connection in her design.”

Orifice stands two feet tall and is shaped like a potato. She has pink, weatherproof rubber skin, and large blue eyes that blink regularly and can also be remotely operated. Her dangling tongue, tuft of Troll doll-esque blond hair, and pink ribbon juxtaposed with an occasional top hat give her a quirky, sometimes unsettling charm.

And yes, her name is literal: She has what Polidoro calls a “functional orifice” on her rear end, a common feature of the artist’s work.

For a city that has officially embraced the grotesque hockey mascot Gritty, which The Guardian has described as “an acid trip of a mascot,” Orifice almost seems like a love letter on wheels. And so far, the reaction to Orifice has been largely positive, Polidoro says.

“Most people smile, laugh and record her, asking questions and wanting to know more,” she says. “Occasionally, people are surprised or find her a bit unsettling. But even those people seem curious and lighthearted.”

Unlike the delivery bots, which Polidoro describes as “something from a science fiction novel,” Orifice seems to hit a sweet spot in terms of adorableness.

“She seems to tap into the psychology of cuteness, like a dog or a child, something understood to be precious and innocent,” she says. (Fortunately, Orifice seems to stop short of provoking “cute aggression,” the urge to squeeze something unbearably adorable.)

And there truly is a psychology of cuteness. Researchers have found that robots designed with the “baby schema” (big eyes, round faces, and other infant-like features) are perceived as cuter and more trustworthy. Research by Kate Darling, a robot ethicist who studied human/robot interaction at the MIT Media Lab for about 15 years, has also found that names and backstories make people hesitate to hurt robots, Dames notes. But a pretty face can only go so far.

“A face alone doesn’t protect a robot,” Dames says. “The Uber Eats rovers have blinking, winking eyes and still got hazed, and a face engineered to look friendly can read as manipulative.”

And that’s not the only way making a robot more “humanlike” can backfire, according to Dr. Donald Hantula, a professor of psychology and neuroscience at Temple University. He likens it to Star Wars’ beloved beep-bot R2-D2 versus the frequently beleaguered, all-too-humanoid C-3PO. The more a robot resembles us, he suggests, the more likely it is to face the bullying we usually reserve for one another.

“The problem is that the more human something becomes, the more we tend to treat it as human-like,” he says. “And we humans have a really terrible history of how we treat fellow members of our species.”

So, will Orifice manage to strike the perfect balance between human and machine, serving as an ambassador of goodwill between tech-weary humans and the machines rolling into their neighborhoods?

So, what is driving hostility toward robots on the street? Whatever it is, Philadelphia seems to have a lot of it. In 2015, hitchBOT, a robot built for a social experiment on the kindness of strangers, was found decapitated at the top of Old City’s Elfreth’s Alley. Named after local blacksmith and property owner Jeremiah Elfreth, the alley is considered one of the oldest continuously inhabited residential streets in the United States.

The alley’s museum has since hosted events about hitchBOT’s demise that are part commemoration, part celebration, with an annual festival possibly in the works.

But hostility toward robots is far from just a Philly thing, or even a delivery bot thing, Dames notes.

“I’d expect friction wherever robots enter public space without local say,” he told Popular Science.

That invasion of space without public buy-in can be especially challenging on crowded public sidewalks, which robots must share, as Dames puts it, “with kids, dogs, wheelchair users, and tourists, none of whom opted in.” On those packed sidewalks, the robots are, in Hantula’s words, “essentially beer coolers on wheels” and consistently “get in people’s way.”

What happens when driverless cars break the law?

The surprising science behind Japan’s vending machine obsession

Can apps really make you fluent in another language?

Do water picks really work? Dentists weigh in.

Beyond these logistical issues, Dames says, robots on the street are often among the only physical manifestations of the otherwise inaccessible corporate entities they represent.

“The robot is the only part of a company a person can physically reach,” he says. “You can’t confront a cloud algorithm or a board of directors, but you can confront the rover on your block.”

Facing down delivery bots is precisely what Dr. Carolyn Marvin, a retired University of Pennsylvania communications professor, has been doing. On Oct. 14, Marvin was arrested and cited for disorderly conduct after stationing herself in front of an Uber Eats robot near Reading Terminal Market. It wasn’t the first time she’d blocked the bots on her daily walks.

Marvin told KYW Newsradio that she’s protesting “the ruthless way that rich corporations are casting aside human workers.” She says she has no intention of stopping.

Confronting robots may also offer an outlet for modern anxieties about the rapid, seemingly ungovernable ascent of artificial intelligence, which some experts have warned could wipe out humanity within a decade. Some passersby have even been known to yell “clanker,” an insult popularized by Star Wars that has since become an anti-AI slur, at Uber Eats bots.

“The physical embodiment gives you a better target in a sense,” Hantula says. “It’s really hard to kick against AI, but it’s like, okay, I see that thing and I can.”

And some of it might be simply for show, Hantula notes.

“A lot of this I think is also driven by getting social media likes,” he says.

Yet some cultures do seem able to coexist peacefully with delivery bots and other robots. In Korea and Japan, for example, robots are viewed as reliable, time-saving helpers, not professional rivals or harbingers of doom.

Orifice probably won’t be able to single-handedly repair the troubled history of robot/human relations in America, but perhaps her presence will provide, as her name suggests, an opening.

“Sometimes it can be, and needs to be, as simple as a moment of raw discovery and joy, savoring the little things that make us human,” Polidoro says. “I’d like Orifice to bring a piece of that [joy and discovery] to everyone she encounters.”

Late drama is icing on cake as Eastbourne Borough and Ramsgate serve up non-league classic

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

For 89 minutes, a huge Priory Lane crowd had enjoyed high-paced action, goalmouth incidents with both sides seeking every which way to gain that crucial breakthrough: the two teams had matched each other stride for stride, tackle for tackle, shot for shot. Borough had edged it, but could not quite make the pressure pay.

A share of the points would not have been unfair: the Rams are an experienced outfit, welded into a tight unit by experienced manager Adie Pinnock, and their recent form has matched Borough's. As home supporters are realising, there are no easy rides in this competitive Isthmian League.

Did you know with an ad-lite subscription to SussexWorld, you get 70% fewer ads while viewing the news that matters to you.

The home side made an eager, front-foot start, with Darren McQueen swerving infield from the right to test Rams keep Jonathan Henly, who saved at full stretch for a Borough corner. The two successive flag-kicks from Kai Corbett were flimsily dealt with by the visitors, but their goal stayed intact.

Corbett has been a notable success story this season: the slumped shoulders of last season are replaced by a spring in the step, and he has the pace, control and strike power to cause havoc in Isthmian defences. Sent away down the left touchline, he turned two defenders inside out before cutting in and hitting a low shot which Henley managed to smother.

The action switched to the River End, where Ramsgate attack had Borough scrambling, but the final strike on Lewis Carey's goal skidded wide of the left post. It was a useful signal, though, that the Rams - and their noisily cheerful supporters - were not here just for a day out.

The first half continued with Borough dominating possession and using it swiftly and creatively, but meeting stubborn defensive resistance from the Rams. Both sets of supporters were in full voice and loving it: a proper non-league occasion in sharp autumn sunshine.

The dressing-room refreshment and managerial pep-talks had sent Borough out with renewed energy for the second half, and they stormed forward for several minutes' worth of relentless attacks. The Rams simply could not get the ball.

From a 35-yard free-kick, headed down in front of goal, Josh Rees was denied by keeper Henley's brave reflex save. Then an audacious overlap by the outstanding Matt Hayden was cleared, and as we passed the hour mark, the Rams' tactics were turning towards holding out for the draw.

That final half-hour was absorbing: a contest of nerves, of concentration, of inventive attacks and defiant resistance.

But this Eastbourne squad do not settle for a draw, and neither do the management team. There was renewed energy from the substitutes - including Shay Harrison.

And with the big red clock showing 89 minutes, Harrison made his mark: latching on to a perfect pass from Sam Oguntayo, he turned on the edge of the box, took a swerving touch forward, and drilled his shot low into the bottom left corner of the Rams' net for 1-0.

Despair for the visitors, tension released and joy unleashed for the home team. Into stoppage time, Harrison went one better: catching Henley stranded, he contrived an outrageous upper-and-downer over the 6ft goalkeeper to double the score. You will not see many better goals this season, in the Isthmian League or the Premier League.

"I can't believe what I'm seeing!" yelled Eastbourne's admirable new young commentator, Bobbie Randall. You'd better believe it, Bobbie: this Borough team is on a journey, and it is bringing back the fun, the entertainment and the habit of winning football matches.

The petty joy of a blue election night

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Ten years ago, watching the dawning of the Trump era on Fox News felt appropriate somehow. Notice that I didn’t say it felt right; after all, we were sinking into an anti-Enlightenment Age propped up by Rupert Murdoch’s propaganda machine. Nothing about any of that felt right.

Still, once the phrase “President-elect Donald Trump” became as inevitable as Thanos, and Fox’s anchors giggled like ascendant supervillains, staring into that snarling maw seemed like the responsible thing to do.

Training my focus on CNN and MS NOW in time to catch 2026’s predicted blue wave promised to be just as illuminating. Back in 2016, while everyone else on CNN struggled to make sense of what was happening, commentator Van Jones somberly characterized the results as “whitelash against a changing country.”

Shortly after Trump was inaugurated, he turned against CNN full force, branding it and every other outlet denying favorable coverage as fake news. Trump’s 2024 victory further endangered any media outlets outside of the right-wing echo chamber, as he hit news organizations with obscenely ridiculous legal complaints to force them to bend the knee.

So we’ll pardon Jones for his deliciously petty reaction to what Jake Tapper, the emcee of CNN’s “Election Night in America” telecast, awkwardly described as a “blue-nami.” By the time the Democrats took control of the House and Senate, the previously funereal Anderson Cooper was gleefully eager to hear what Jones had to say. And his colleague did not disappoint, delivering what may have been the night’s most satisfying monologue.

“If you join a cult, at some point the cult leader takes you to Jonestown and tries to get you to drink the Kool-Aid,” he observed as chastened GOP pundit Scott Jennings sat inches away. “This is Jonestown for the Republican Party. They drank the Kool-Aid, and they have candidates that are dropping like flies across the country because they did not stand up to this guy when he was doing things that were wrong . . .

“You join a cult, at some point, you go to Jonestown,” Jones repeated in closing. “That’s where you are tonight, Republicans.” Then he pretended to pass a cup to his Trumpian counterpart.

Election night news coverage is a stressful affair in the best of times. In 2026, CNN and MS NOW, the channel formerly known as MSNBC, entered the fray as battered underdogs. MS NOW is now owned by Versant, a group of cable channels spun off from NBCUniversal. CNN, part of Warner Bros. Discovery, will soon be owned by Skydance and its Trump-allied owner David Ellison.

Neither newsroom can claim relief over what’s on the horizon, which is why Cooper led CNN’s coverage with the air of an undertaker greeting democracy’s mourners. America’s major news outlets have been taking it on the chin with the rest of us — including Cooper, a former “60 Minutes” correspondent who fled Bari Weiss’ CBS takeover only for Ellison to come for his cable news shire anyway. Blue wave or not, CNN’s future is uncertain.

So although MS NOW may be scrappier and looser these days, at least its reporters and pundits aren’t working through an institutional existential crisis. That fatigue showed up in how CNN’s Magic Wall wrangler, John King, lived cautiously from moment to moment, allowing only a mild display of wonder as he shifted between the largely scarlet 2024 map and the much bluer 2026 version.

“What’s happening right now?” King wondered. “What’s the right word? Is it a rebuke? Is it repudiation? Is it shellacking? Is it a spanking? I mean, at the moment, this is the collapse of the president’s party on live television as you watch it play out.”

And for the first time in a decade of watching election nights, it was a joy to experience — like learning how to hope again.

Peddling optimism isn’t the job of any journalistic organization, of course. But knowing what CNN’s newsroom is contending with regardless of this election outcome makes their composure especially admirable. The CNN team had a 10-ton weight pressing down on them and still managed to convey the sense of celebration presumably gripping most of its viewers.

Most people tune out election night noise regardless of whether the vote goes in their favor, perhaps gleaning what they can from the flashy graphics and chyrons. This gave MS NOW’s Ali Velshi an advantage at the network’s Big Board now that his former co-worker Steve Kornacki has moved over to NBC. Both have earned fandoms by injecting levity into managing each election’s data flood.  On Tuesday night, Kornacki inspired a meme simply by being captured on his new network’s Kornacki Cam as he walked down a hall.

But Velshi kept his version of his predecessor’s two-step going, buzzing around on MS NOW’s Big Board with a hummingbird’s energy. “Annie Andrews is outperforming, and Darlene Graham is underperforming,” he rapidly blurted, breaking down the Democratic challenger’s road to her unexpected win in South Carolina over the late Republican Sen. Lindsey Graham’s sister, before turning to the camera with a delightfully bemused look to say, “This is a totally weird conversation that we are having. Right?” It’s true. After years of polls overstating the Democrats’ chances, it did feel strange to have underestimated how much they’d win.

Still, Rachel Maddow‘s ebullience from the moment MS NOW’s coverage began was tough to beat. Before a single result came in, she cheerfully offered, “So far, the one thing we can definitively say is that Americans like our democracy, and they are enjoying using it in record numbers in this cycle.”

Once Democrats claimed majorities in the House and Senate a few hours later, she let loose. In calling the race for Pennsylvania’s 8th congressional district for Democratic Scranton Mayor Paige Cognetti, Maddow couldn’t resist calling her Republican opponent, Rob Bresnahan, a “sometime ghost hunter” as her colleagues snickered in the background.

This is an understandable reaction to voters’ referendum on the president’s unpopular and economically detrimental policies. Americans, regardless of partisan stance, are struggling to afford the basics. But as Chris Hayes pointed to the shock of so many Democratic upsets, he noted that high prices aren’t the only thing forcing this drastic change. “The rot that is coming from the top of the country is very, very, very clearly on voters’ minds,” he said.

So if Jones wants to indulge in a little grave dancing, so be it.

It was even better to see other correspondents take stock of all the history made on Tuesday night. New Hampshire voters chose a Democrat, Chris Pappas, to join the U.S. Senate, making him the first out gay man elected to the chamber.

Voters turned Texas blue and elected four Black governors – including former Atlanta mayor Keisha Lance Bottoms, who became the first woman and the first Black person to be elected governor in Georgia, and the first female Black governor in our country’s history.

Once that state’s contest for governor was called for Bottoms, MS NOW’s team tapped Symone Sanders-Townsend to contextualize what that meant for voters and Black women. Sanders-Townsend reminded us that when Trump calls Georgia to gripe, it will be Bottoms on the other end of the line. She’ll also be the one to ensure the 2028 election is certified for the rightful winner.

Want more from culture than just the latest trend? The Swell highlights art made to last. Sign up here

But when Sanders-Townsend’s colleagues asked her to share how Bottoms’ victory made her feel, her reaction hit home. “After the 2024 presidential election, for myself and Black women all over this country, it felt like a repudiation of us,” she said, holding back tears. “…There were Black women, myself included, that felt like, ‘Well, maybe America just does not want us.'”

So to Sanders-Townsend, and all the other Black women disappointed by their fellow citizens in 2024, Bottoms’ victory said, “Georgia wanted Keisha.”

That moment didn’t diminish Jones’ take on what this election night meant to him. “This is like Christmas and a birthday and an Easter bunny and Halloween!” he said. “This is the repudiation of Trumpism that people have been hoping for, seeing American democracy stand up.”

But by allowing herself to cry on camera, Sanders-Townsend broadcast a different kind of signal. She let us know that on this night, and for the first time in a long time, it was OK to ease into some sense of relief. And that’s better than the fleeting pleasure of retaliatory pettiness, well-earned though it may be.

Bayern star creeping up on sublime Barcelona duo in WhoScored rankings for 2026-27

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Who are the best-performing players across Europe’s top five leagues this season? We’ve turned to their WhoScored ratings to find out.

WhoScored‘s comprehensive system of player ratings is based on a whole host of stats, which give an indication of who’s playing well and who’s not.

As the season progresses, we’ll be keeping track of the top 10 highest-rated players in Europe’s top five leagues. Right now, there’s no one from the Premier League…

Bellingham had a brightish start to the season with Real Madrid and got himself back among the scorers at the weekend against Racing Santander in his highest-rated performance of the season so far.

Sinayoko’s numbers have swelled in recent weeks. Of his eight goals and five assists this season for Paris FC, four each have come in his last three games.

Unsurprisingly, he’s been the highest-rated player in two of them.

Stuttgart lost their last two matches before the break, but Mittelstadt received a man-of-the-match rating in both of them. It wouldn’t be a massive shock to see the 29-year-old Germany international move on in the summer if the Swabians regress to the mean and fail to qualify for Europe again.

It’s been a decent start to the season for Werder Bremen and Austrian midfielder Grull has stood out.

He both scored and assisted, with a man-of-the-match rating, as they defeated RB Leipzig and Augsburg at the Weser Stadium. Grull even came out of their opening weekend 4-1 tonking at Freiburg with credit, earning an 8.01 match rating in the heavy loss.

He was the highest-rated player against Borussia Dortmund when they got back from the international break and has made an assist in each of his last two game.s

Quite simply incredible last season and seeing little reason to change that, Kane has four goals and six assists so far in the Bundesliga this season.

Since the international break, he’s been more about the assists (five) than the goals (one) in the league, but it just shows he really can do it all.

RB Leipzig prospect Nusa has four goals and six assists to his name so far this season. We’re pretty sure the big clubs will be circling.

He was the match winner on Halloween against Schalke with a goal and assist in a 2-0 win.

Still only 21, the winger has a big future ahead of him.

Mbappe came second for the Ballon d’Or this year, but mission 2027 is well underway.

A hat-trick against Real Sociedad in Madrid’s second game of the season showed Mbappe means business after a summer in which he became the World Cup’s leading scorer of all time.

The French forward has also scored braces against Rayo Vallecano, Sevilla and Barcelona, helping him creep up on the leaders here.

By last season’s standards, Olise had a bit of a quiet start to 2026-27. Just one goal and zero assists in their first three Bundesliga outings of the campaign, which included a goalless draw at newly-promoted Schalke and a narrow 2-1 victory over minnows Elversberg.

But his 10.0 masterclass in the Champions League against Bodo/Glimt (two goals and an assist) hinted that last term was no flash in the pan. He remains one of Europe’s most devastating, elite forwards. A hat-trick and another 10.0 rating in a 7-0 mauling of Union Berlin catapulted him into the top five, and a goal-and-assist performance against RB Leipzig lifted him higher into the top three.

Back in January, we declared Raphinha the best player in the world, asking the question:

“Is there another player in world football who can claim to elevate their team as much as Raphinha? In a collective sport, isn’t that the most important thing?”

That take didn’t age all that well, as the Brazilian suffered a frustratingly injury-ravaged latter half of the 2025-26 campaign and struggled to make an impact at the World Cup as a result.

But look at him now. After being converted into a striker, Raphinha has been on fire this season. Fifteen(!) goals and three assists in La Liga so far, averaging a goal contribution every 40 minutes.

Barca’s failure to win that game knocked his rating down a bit here, but he’s still top of the tree.

And yep, he’s still only 19. This lad is ridiculous. He has completed the most dribbles across Europe.

Nathan Jones confirms worry over ‘tough’ injury setback for Charlton Athletic summer signing

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Nathaniel Chalobah made his first start for Charlton Athletic since the end of August on Tuesday night. However, he only lasted until half time following a crunching challenge from Akin Famewo.

And, unfortunately for CAFC, it appears that the tackle aggravated the thigh injury that kept Chalobah out for two months. Speaking to South London Sport, Nathan Jones said: “He got injured. I think it is the same thing as what kept him out at the start of the season – his thigh.

“We will have the diagnosis, but it wasn’t a tactical decision, we won every game with him at the start of the season and then he got injured. We tried to bring him back – he has trained with us for nearly two weeks so it is not too early. We were always going to give him 55-60 minutes anyway, but I think he has pulled his thigh. It is a tough one again.“

Chalobah’s last start came against Preston North End, a game Charlton Athletic won 1-0. The Addicks won their first three games of the season with the former Chelsea and Sheffield Wednesday man in midfield, but they have not won since.

Chalobah has gained a wealth of experience in the Championship, but he has often struggled with injuries. Previous club Sheffield Wednesday spent large spells without the 31-year-old, and the same fate looks to have hit the Addicks. He played only 31 times in a two-year stay at Hillsborough.

Chalobah’s most notable injury came during his time at Watford, when he had major knee surgery in 2018 and spent a total of 340 days sidelined.

Fears are that his injury struggles are set to persist at CAFC. His influence is clear to see as a strong defensive presence in the middle of the park, but the likes of Conor Coventry, Oliver Skipp and potentially the rarely-seen Timothy Ouma will have to cover in his absence.

In the summer, Charlton Athletic recruited some experienced Championship including midfielders Chalobah and Skipp. However, the former is dealing with injuries, and the latter is yet to win a game in CAFC colours.

They bolstered their forward line with Millenic Alli and Karlan Grant. Grant has scored two goals this season, however, Alli is yet to score and both are being outshone by academy graduate Tyreece Campbell – who has not scored in the last five league matches himself.

The Addicks need more from their summer signings, with disappointment over Chalobah’s lack of fitness and the limited impacts of bigger names like Grant, Skipp and more.

Charlton Athletic have some tough matches on the horizon, with Sheffield United, Wolves, Lincoln City and Blackburn Rovers to play before the end of the month. Time will tell just how long Chalobah faces out, but Jones has to find a winning formula with his available players, otherwise his future at The Valley will be in doubt as their winless run stretches to 11.

10 Tips for Hunting Pheasants Without a Dog

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Before I ever owned a gun dog, I walked up pheasants on the family farm by myself. While I wouldn’t go back to hunting without a dog, I remember the special thrill of dogless hunting: there’s no warning before you see the bird. Every flush is a jump-scare, especially when those roosters burst out of the grass almost directly beneath your feet.

Dogless pheasant hunting was common in the days of high pheasant numbers. Solo hunters walked fencelines and road ditches. Gangs teamed up to drive and block cornfields. Those tactics still work with the right cover, but you’ll walk far more miles between pheasants than your parents and grandparents ever did. They had enough birds to take pheasants for granted. You’ll work for every rooster you scare up, and you’ll want to treat it like the trophy it is. Here are ten tips for hunting pheasants without a dog.

Small covers, like brushy waterways, center-pivot field corners, draws, road ditches (where legal), and fencelines are a dogless hunter’s best bet. Pheasants run, they sit tight, and they fly. You need them to fly. If you can push them to an edge, they’re out of running room, and they’re more likely to slip up and flush. Start at the wider end, if there is one, and push to the narrow end. Always imagine you are driving pheasants ahead of you, because you probably are.

Dogless hunters don’t get as many chances as hunters with dogs. That’s a simple truth. You have to make the most of every opportunity. All that money you save by not paying vet bills and buying dog food? Spend it at the range. Trapshooting is good practice for the straightaways and quartering shots that make up the majority of the chances you’ll get in the field. Skeet teaches you how to hit all the angles, and it’s a game better suited to using your hunting gun. Shooting from a portable trap is good practice, too, and it lets you focus on keeping your eye on the target and your head on the gun.

Compared to most other upland game birds, pheasants aren’t hard to shoot. They are hard to kill. When you hunt without a dog, you need your birds to hit the ground dead. There’s no retriever to bail you out if you only break a wing. The best shots at pheasants are close, going-away shots, and quartering or crossing shots where the head, neck, and vitals are exposed. Ideally, you want to shoot pheasants between a little less than 20 and about 30 yards. Long, going-away shots risk a poorly hit running bird. I don’t even take that shot with a dog, especially in heavy cover.

Dogless hunters don’t have the early warning systems that a flusher’s buzzing tail or a pointer’s rock-solid point provide. When you hunt without a dog, pheasants can pop up at any time. You can’t react well to the flush if you’re trudging along looking at the toes of your boots. Be on constant alert. That doesn’t mean strangling the gun in a death grip and jumping at every sound. It means your eyes are always up, you’re carrying your gun comfortably in a way you can get it into action, and you’re visualizing a pheasant in the air. I used to liken the alertness you need for walking in a field to driving a car, where you watch the road with part of your mind alert that something might happen fast, while also carrying on a conversation or listening to the radio.

Don’t kid yourself. Pheasants almost always know right where you are. Still, there’s no need to advertise your presence any more. Dogless hunters do have the advantage of stealth because they aren’t yelling at dogs. Ease the truck door shut. Keep talking to a minimum. The one time you can beat a pheasant’s hearing is on a very windy day when everything rustles. Sometimes, days like that make them impossibly skittish, but other times, you can get right up on top of them in the wind.

In the days of bountiful pheasant numbers, hunters often formed big groups. Many didn’t bother with dogs, or if they brought them, they used them primarily for pick-up duty. A large party can hunt big set-aside fields effectively. Detail several hunters to serve as blockers. They go to the far end of the field and space themselves along the cover. The larger half of the group walks from the other end. The drivers should stay in a straight line for safety and move toward the blockers. Some birds will flush along the way, and if you do this right, pheasants will be in the air everywhere as the drivers get close to the edge of the field. Obviously, safety is extremely important, and you can’t shoot low birds in a hunt like this. If you can see the sky below the rooster’s belly, you can safely shoot.

A big field presents a daunting challenge for one or two dogless hunters. Chasing pheasants in a large grass field is like trying to grab minnows in a bucket. To trap them, walk from the inside of the field out to the corners. If you push them ahead of you, they will have to sit or flush (of course, they might just loop back around you in the grass. No one said dogless hunting was easy). But your odds go up if you can corner the birds. Working the field edges is always worthwhile, too, as you might catch a bird that’s moving back and forth from food to cover as it lingers too close to the edge.

Pheasants hear a steady drumbeat of footsteps and hunker down to let danger pass by. They have excellent hearing, and they have bones in their feet that sense vibration. You want to make them uneasy. The way to do that is to pause periodically. If they don’t know where you are, they may get nervous and flush. Vary your pace, too. You want to get into their heads.

Any time you reach an edge or corner, or meet a blocker, don’t relax immediately. Birds might be crouched right there. Give them a minute or more to flush. Just last fall, I met my hunting partner, who was blocking, at the top of a terraced hillside. I am not sure what our dogs were doing, but they weren’t there, and we stood, chatting, guns broken open for several minutes before a rooster flushed at my feet and flew off.

Two or three hunters can borrow the hammer and anvil tactics of big groups on a small scale. As I mentioned, you always imagine you’re pushing running pheasants ahead of you. A blocker at the end of a piece of cover sometimes prevents those birds from sneaking out the back door or flushing wild. Give your blocker time to work into position, then drive toward them, pausing and zig-zagging as you go.

When you shoot a bird, mark where it fell as best you can, then go straight to it. Dead pheasants can stay alive just long enough to bury themselves in the grass, and you’ll want to know exactly where to search. Dead pheasants can also come back to life. With about fifteen minutes left in the last day of last season, I shot my final pheasant of the year. It fell on the other side of a stout woven-wire fence and post-holed into a snowbank. The tail feathers stuck up out of the hole, and when I saw that, I thought it would make a great end-of-the-season picture. I decided to hunt the last few minutes of the year, then go back for my photo and my bird.

When I got there, the pheasant was gone. If I had been hunting dogless, as I did so many years ago, the bird would have gotten away. Zeke found it, and I relearned the lesson that any hunter, especially hunters without dogs, needs to go to their birds right away.

Two Movements, One Striking Watch From Louis Erard × Vianney Halter

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

It’s a good day to be a watch enthusiast. The Louis Erard × Vianney Halter Futur Antérieur was recently unveiled—a limited-edition release that’s equal parts striking and unexpected. Despite this being the third collaboration between Louis Erard and Vianney Halter, the latest watch takes a decidedly different approach.

The Futur Antérieur differs dramatically from its predecessors. Where past editions were essentially reinterpretations of an existing Louis Erard model, decorated with Vianney Halter’s signature design elements, this new release is far more elaborate, appearing instead as an adaptation of one of Halter’s iconic steampunk-inspired designs. The porthole-style dial windows, gold-plated rivets on the porthole bezels and crowns, and even the dial and hand configurations all reference the perpetual calendar Vianney Halter launched in 1998, which costs more than $200,000.

Now, at a list price of about $6,000 USD (before taxes), the Futur Antérieur is obviously not a perpetual calendar, and its split dial needs a little explanation. The complete separation between its hour-and-minute dial and its running seconds dial is achieved through a rather unorthodox but straightforward solution: Each dial is driven by a separate Sellita SW100 automatic movement. This allows for both a unique time display and the ability to keep its case remarkably thin at just 8.85 mm.

Speaking of dimensions, the proportions of this watch are impressive across the board. Its case measures a modest 38 mm in diameter, and articulating lugs let the Futur Antérieur conform to a wide range of wrist sizes. After spending some hands-on time with the watch, I quickly realized something unexpected: It wears incredibly well on a single-pass fabric or NATO strap, which you wouldn’t necessarily expect given its design.

During my time with the watch, one key question came up. If it’s fitted with two movements, why not offer it as a dual-time watch? Personally, I like the choice of a running seconds hand, and to me, the decision seemed to echo the Antiqua it was based on more faithfully. With the Antiqua, each porthole displayed something clearly different, and having a second similar dial with another time zone would seem like too great a departure from the intended vision.

According to Louis Erard, the decision was a touch more practical. The Sellita SW100, despite its compact size, is not the most accurate or easily regulated Swiss automatic movement. Thus, getting the two movements to run in harmony would be too difficult at the price point the brand aimed for, and displaying running seconds allows far more leeway for differences in accuracy between the movements.

Now, before anyone gets too up in arms about this, look at how much watch you’re getting for the price of entry. It’s a statement piece, and if you want greater accuracy, that equivalent dollar figure is best spent on one of Grand Seiko’s Spring Drive watches or a Tudor with METAS certification (with a stated accuracy of 0 to +5 seconds per day).

My one gripe in all this? These are very scarce, and likely long gone from the primary market. Louis Erard is only making 178 of each dial configuration, along with 78 collector’s box sets containing both watches for CHF 9,900 (approximately $12,075).

From Montreal and Vancouver to New York and Toronto, Justin Mastine-Frost's career has been shaped by a lifelong curiosity and time spent on the road. He began in photography before working in construction and later becoming a certified BMW technician, a background that combines visual storytelling, hands-on craftsmanship, and technical problem-solving. After several years covering the automotive industry, his passion for collecting naturally led him into the world of watches. His work has appeared in Esquire, Bloomberg, Highsnobiety, Road & Track, and Robb Report, among other publications. He has also contributed to several books on watchmaking, including Millennium Watch Book, Sea Time, Bulova: A History of Firsts, and Bvlgari Beyond Time. Based in Toronto, he is Director of Digital Content at SHARP Magazine.

Court hears argument in religious discrimination dispute

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

Justice Thomas Johnson was born on this day in 1732. As we noted in a Closer Look about Johnson in July, he has the distinction of holding the third-shortest tenure of any associate justice in Supreme Court history.

On Tuesday, the justices heard argument in St. Mary Catholic Parish v. Roy, a religious discrimination challenge brought by Catholic preschools in Colorado over the state’s requirement that all schools participating in its universal preschool program must admit everyone – including LGBTQ children and children with LGBTQ parents.

Today, the justices will hear argument in Guerrero v. Johnson, on the limited circumstances in which a state prisoner can return to federal court for a second round of post-conviction review.

On Friday, the justices will meet in a private conference to discuss cases and vote on petitions for review. Orders from Friday’s conference are expected on Monday at 9:30 a.m. EST.

On Oct. 21, the Trump administration asked the justices to allow it to resume construction of its border wall in the Big Bend region of southwestern Texas. The request is now fully briefed, and the court’s decision could come at any time.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/what-does-blue-wave-mean-healthcare-policy" hreflang="en">What Democratic control of Congress means for healthcare in 2027</a>

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →
What Democratic control of Congress means for healthcare in 2027" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Tuesday’s forecast-busting blue wave puts wind in the sails of Democrats who have promised a stark departure from Trump-era healthcare policy—though whether or not the shifts will be incremental or seismic hinges on intra-party debates and the president’s veto.

As of noon Wednesday, Democrats had flipped seven seats in the Senate and 21 in the House, setting the party up for comfortable majorities as unexpected battleground races continue to be tallied.

Healthcare issues were a major talking point through many midterm election campaigns. In battleground Senate races, for instance, Democrats spent $65 million on ads primarily focused on healthcare, more than twice that of their next-most prominent topic. Healthcare was overall the second-most prominent issue featured in ads (as measured by spend) across the two parties, behind a push on taxation topics from Republicans.

And within those ads: promises to deliver lower costs, greater coverage and federal oversight, starting Jan. 1, 2027.

The immediate legislative impact will be seen in the bills new chamber leaders bring to the floor.

A health policy agenda circulated in September by a working group under Rep. Hakeem Jeffries (D-N.Y.)—the House’s current minority leader and likely speaker in the next Congress—outlined immediate, “First 100 Hours” goals that include repeal of the Medicaid coverage and funding cuts of 2025’s H.R. 1 (the One Big Beautiful Bill Act) as well as restoration of enhanced Affordable Care Act premium tax credits that expired at the top of 2026 and vastly increased marketplace plan premiums.

Other 100-hours priorities outlined in the document include medical debt protections, removal of visa program fees for healthcare workers, an end to an AI prior authorization program in Traditional Medicare, restoration of withheld National Institutes of Health funding, a block on immigration enforcement operations in hospitals and guaranteed no-cost coverage of recommended vaccines regardless of changes in federal recommendations.

Additionally, the new majorities would install key Democratic policy heads to congressional committees responsible for advancing healthcare legislation and conducting oversight into government and private sector conduct. Among those new chairs would be Vermont’s Bernie Sanders, a vocal opponent of corporate interests in healthcare and the country’s chief Medicare for All advocate, who would chair the Senate Health, Education, Labor and Pensions (HELP) Committee; and Oregon’s Ron Wyden, who as Finance Committee ranking member recently pursued oversight of Medicaid stakeholders, endorsed Sanders’ Medicare for All bill, led calls to hold “Big Insurance” accountable for administrative roadblocks to care and summarized his midterms reaction with a single word: “Subpoenas.”

Underpinning the party’s legislative leaders are a slew of new-to-office healthcare advocates. Several of those elected to Congress have backgrounds as physicians or other COVID-era public health experience. Among the standouts are Ohio Governor-elect Amy Acton, a pediatrician who was director of the state’s Department of Health and resigned early in the pandemic in protest of state leadership; and Michigan Senator-elect Abdul El-Sayed, an epidemiologist and Detroit’s former health director, who has made progressive health policy goals like Medicare for All a cornerstone of his campaign.

For the public and the healthcare industry, the key question is how Democrats will pursue their healthcare agenda under a divided government and lame-duck president.

Molly Turco, who covers healthcare policy at TD Cowen Washington Research Group, wrote in a Wednesday policy note that “with Trump in the [White House], passage of major reforms are very unlikely and even medium-sized policies, like rollbacks of Medicaid [state-directed payment] cuts or restoring ACA subsidies, are an uphill battle. But the midterm results and catalysts like the debt ceiling give Ds leverage.”

Consultants at BRG, in a media roundtable late last week, generally agreed with the veto hurdle, but noted that a stymied Congress still has room to write and pass bills that could become new policy in a future majority, as was the case with Medicaid work requirements pushed by Republicans during the end of the Biden administration but ultimately codified last year.

There is still an avenue for dealmaking between the parties, analysts and advisors noted, such as on areas with general bipartisan agreement (think this year’s pharmacy benefit manager reforms, or other major pain points like physician pay or surprise billing arbitration reform) as part of must-pass spending packages slated for this December and the coming years. The president, for his part, told outlets late Tuesday night that he intends to work with Democrats to pass bills he believes will benefit the country.

PricewaterhouseCoopers, in a Wednesday policy note, said “targeted bipartisan legislation is most likely to move. Look for action on cybersecurity, AI, price transparency, modernizing drug development and prior authorization processes. Regardless of congressional gridlock, continued regulatory implementation will shape near-term business and compliance requirements.”

Turco’s note predicts a dual messaging strategy for congressional Democrats on affordability and accountability, with the latter involving “an aggressive corporate investigation and hearings strategy.”

The TD Cowen analyst also questioned whether Democrats’ midterm over-performance and gains by progressive candidates will embolden the party to pursue and campaign on “bold” healthcare reforms like Medicare for All ahead of 2028.

Wyden’s recent endorsement of Sanders’ reform bill “is a powerful signal that even long-tenured moderates are under pressure to have a vision that reimagines [healthcare],” Turco wrote, though “many other D members are worried about blowback from M4A support that has almost no chance of being a viable policy. These members are vocal about their concerns and want to work on more realistic policies like restoring the ACA and Medicaid, building on IRA negotiations, reducing premiums/ deductibles for employer-based coverage, adding more benefits to Medicare, etc.”

Investment banking firm William Blair’s equity research team, in an election day brief, noted that Democratic governorships bring immediate impact to the healthcare industry as states make strategic decisions on how to implement, or offset, the provisions of H.R. 1.

The limited avenues for swiftly rolling back that sweeping bill mean that industry players, including Medicare Advantage plans and margin-strapped hospitals, should begin preparing for its financial headwinds, consultants and health systems heads have said. But that doesn’t mean Democrats’ strong night has dashed all hopes for rollbacks or other tweaks to ease the pain.

“While the election has changed who controls Congress, it doesn't change the financial challenges hospitals will face in 2027,” Chris Van Gorder, president and CEO of nonprofit integrated health system Scripps Health, said in a statement reacting to midterm election results. “Democrats won't have the votes to simply reverse major federal health care cuts over a presidential veto. But Congress is far from powerless. Lawmakers can use their oversight and budget authority to press for changes in how those cuts are carried out, protect critical health care funding where possible and work across party lines to ease the growing financial pressures on hospitals and impact on patients."

Even if political gridlock takes hold, Tuesday’s results ensure that affordability will be center stage in Washington, analysts said, especially as consumers’ cost pressures will likely persist well through the 2028 election cycle.

“Even where legislation stalls, don’t dismiss the headlines,” PwC advised healthcare executives. “Democratic hearings, investigations and proposed bills offer insight into the policies taking shape for 2028, while growing pressure around affordability continues to push the healthcare market toward consumer expectations. Executives who look beyond near-term political conflict, plan for multiple policy scenarios and adapt to evolving consumer demands will be better positioned for potential healthcare reform after 2028.”

On the virtual care front, ATA Action, the advocacy-focused affiliate of the American Telemedicine Association, said the election presents an opportunity to build on strong bipartisan support for telehealth and virtual care at the federal and state levels. The group plans to continue working across party lines to make Medicare telehealth policies permanent, expand digital health access and remove regulatory barriers to virtual care, Kyle Zebley, CEO of the ATA and Executive Director of ATA Action, said in a statement.

Nov. 4, 1922: Tutankhamun’s tomb is discovered

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

When archaeologists peered inside Tutankhamun’s tomb a few weeks after its discovery on Nov. 4, 1922, they found antechambers packed to the brim with thousands of artifacts: statues, furniture, jewelry, clothes, chariots, paintings. Among these possessions was an iron dagger — just over one foot in length and crafted from an iron meteorite.

The cosmic source of the iron in the late pharaoh’s dagger was confirmed when a team of Italian researchers analyzed its elemental composition for the first time in 2016. While Earth-based iron contains about 4 percent nickel, the team found that the blade’s higher nickel concentration (and trace amounts of cobalt) was instead consistent with space rock.

Four years later, aided by the Grand Egyptian Museum’s conservation center, Takafumi Matsui, director of the Chiba Institute of Technology’s Planetary Exploration Research Center in Japan, and his colleagues used a portable scanning X-ray fluorescence instrument to map out the elements on the surface of the blade. They found not just iron, nickel, and cobalt, but also chlorine and manganese, among others. They also found that the bumpy, black spots along its edges and center likely originated from troilite — a mineral commonly found in iron meteorites. Further research released in 2022 investigated the how and where the blade was forged after its raw materials fell to Earth.

Marisa Sloan received her master’s degree in health, environment & science reporting from Northwestern University’s Medill School of Journalism, and a chemistry degree from UNC Greensboro.

South Dakota Is Losing Its Hometown Pheasant Hunters. Meanwhile, the Nonresident Business Is Booming

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 4, 2026 1:52 PM EST

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

I was hunting a strip of public walk-in access across the road from a fleet of combines. As the harvesters crawled closer, pheasants kept flushing out of the standing corn and into the chest-high cover I was following my dog through. I marked one rooster that landed 300 yards ahead and, when Hatchet eventually trailed and flushed him, I was ready.

But I never shouldered my gun. The bird flushed straight toward the cab of a grain truck and veered out of range. The man sitting behind the windshield, who looked to be in his 20s, threw up his arms. Worried I might be irritating the farmers despite my restraint, I headed for my truck — the one with the Arkansas plates — with the birds I’d already killed. I know I’m a visitor here, and I don’t want to contribute to an existing problem.

This fall I drove 10 hours from my home to hunt public-land pheasants. It’s my seventh season in a row making the trip, and I can’t remember ever seeing more pickups loaded with kennels and coolers on I-29. We were all South Dakota bound.

As I’ve hunted my tried-and-true public spots and explored new ones this year, I’ve been surprised (though seldom hindered) by the number of fellow hunters getting after it. Each time I pass a truck parked in the ditch, I can’t help but clock the license plate: Minnesota, Wisconsin, Iowa, North Dakota. I’ve yet to spot a South Dakota plate that seems to belong to a pheasant hunter.

Anecdotal though it may be, my experience tracks with a broader trend in South Dakota. Annual harvest surveys show nonresident pheasant hunters are outnumbering resident hunters, and have every year since 2002. Last season approximately 57,100 residents hunted pheasants, while about 82,600 nonresidents hunted pheasants, according to those survey projections from South Dakota Game, Fish and Parks.

And during opening weekend on Oct. 17 and 18, SDGFP conservation officers talked with some 1,800 pheasant hunters. They hailed from 44 different states.

Many South Dakotans worry their home state is too busy courting these nonresident hunters — or more precisely, their wallets — to notice the fading tradition of resident pheasant culture.

“We love out-of-state hunters. And we want everybody to keep coming and enjoying South Dakota’s tradition,” says Brad Johnson, a South Dakota real-estate appraiser and lifelong hunter. “We just want our state government and our state tourism industry to understand the importance of in-state hunters. I think there’s a real strong feeling that we’re being taken for granted.”

Johnson, who lives in Watertown, recently wrote a column in the South Dakota Searchlight that dug into this trend — and struck a nerve with locals. He acknowledges that nonresidents are the “financial backbone” of the state’s pheasant program, thanks to higher license fees. (Residents pay $36 for a season-long small game license and a $10 habitat stamp; nonresidents pay $142 to hunt birds for 10 days, plus a $25 habitat stamp.)

“We understand that [financial dynamic] as South Dakota hunters. But at the same time, we need to start putting more emphasis on growing in-state hunting,” says Johnson. “We put so much of our advertising effort in out-of-state markets that we know where most of the hunters come from. And we don’t do similar types of advertising geared toward local residents and trying to figure out programs that are going to build new hunters within the state.”

DIY public-land pheasant hunting, especially during poor bird years, isn’t for the faint of heart. I’ve had to work for my limits of wild roosters over the seasons, slogging miles through nasty weather, thick cover, cattail sloughs, and rolling hills. Last weekend I hiked 9 miles over public ground with my dog (who covered 15 on his own four legs) and two buddies to fill our three limits. It was one of the best days of pheasant hunting I can remember.

For hunters with more cash and less interest in (or the physical ability for) such a grind, South Dakota offers hundreds of private shooting preserves. These lodges cater to businessmen, casual hunters, and the wealthy — almost exclusively out-of-staters who fly into hubs like Pierre and Aberdeen to shoot high volume put-and-take birds. Business is booming.

In the last quarter century, the number of licensed pheasant preserves across the state has grown substantially, from 157 in 2001 to a record 237 last season. Preserves must be a minimum of 160 acres (and no more than 2,560 acres). And over the past nine seasons, the harvest of released captive pheasants shot on preserves has climbed 50 percent (this excludes incidental harvest of wild birds). During the 2025-26 season, hunters shot about 400,000 pheasants on preserves — a record high.

“Everybody’s got a story about why they don’t hunt now, and all other problems that they’ve had getting access compared to how it used to be,” Johnson says of local hunters. “There’s a shift in emphasis in South Dakota toward more the commercialization of hunting — and I get it. I’ve got a lot of friends who own hunting operations … They’ve got really nice hunting operations. They’re all paid hunting and geared almost 100 percent to out-of-state hunters … Very few people in South Dakota are going to pay for that type of experience.”

Residents simply can’t afford the rates — from $2,000 to $4,000 per day at the fanciest lodges, he says — that traveling hunters are willing to pay. Meanwhile, hunting operations are leasing private land for their clients. This is more of an issue in east-central South Dakota, in the heart of pheasant country.

This steady rise in preserve pheasant hunting also contributes to access fees or the leasing of private land that could otherwise be open for permission to residents. After doing some work for a real-estate client one year, Johnson asked if he could hunt their property.

“They said, ‘Well, is it pheasant hunting? And if it’s pheasant hunting, would you be willing to pay a little bit? If it’s duck hunting, just go ahead,’” says Johnson. “So we value our pheasants quite a bit.”

While the hunter numbers cited in this story focus on wild pheasant hunting, the data they’re drawn from is murky (more on this in a bit). SDGFP explicitly says “no shooting preserve license holders were surveyed and none of their harvest is included” in wild bird harvest estimates. But these annual figures undoubtedly capture some preserve hunts, since hunters can legally shoot pheasants on preserves using their small game licenses.

The state’s harvest reports also don’t include any details on where hunters are killing pheasants or if they’re paying for access.

One lifelong South Dakota hunter, who asked not to be named so as not to upset factions of the hunting community, still puts the permission success rate at about 50 percent near the Pierre area.

“When I was a kid, it was all wild birds. We’d hop on the tailgate, the kids would walk through the [nasty cover], and adults blocked or did the easy stuff. It was opening weekend and it was a tradition. I think that tradition is still there, and I think people want to celebrate it the same way, but it has been commercialized. It wasn’t commercialized when I was a kid. It was good old landowners who let people hunt.”

South Dakota’s most recent push to ramp up pheasant license sales began in 2020 with its “Hunt the Greatest” campaign. The collaboration between the state’s game and tourism agencies spent about $662,000 that year in advertising on cable TV, podcasts like MeatEater, and in paid searches online, among other avenues. (Outdoor Life has also received South Dakota tourism funding for sponsored campaigns.) And in 2024, the campaign targeted South Dakota plus 13 out-of-state markets, including Minnesota, Iowa, Nebraska, Wisconsin, Colorado, Texas, and Michigan.

Neither agency could provide the breakdown of spending by in-state and out-of-state dollars to OL by press time.

The Hunt the Greatest campaign launched the year following the state’s final roadside pheasant counts, in 2019.

“Then-Governor Kristi Noem did away with that [roadside brood count], and that was partly because of the pressure from the tourism industry,” says Johnson. Noem’s family owned a pheasant lodge, and she also described herself as an owner and operator of a hunting lodge. “If the report came out that bird numbers were down, that sometimes caused out-of-state hunters to not come.”

Concluding the best long-running and scientific data collection on the state’s bird numbers was controversial, to say the least. As one former pheasant research biologist with SDGFP put it, the state’s current hunter survey stats are “fake news” that are “not verifiable or scientific.”

“The loss of the brood survey … means we have no accurate information on South Dakota pheasant populations for seven years,” that biologist, Larry Fredrickson, wrote in a letter to the Huron Daily Plainsman this spring. “I hope the state gets the brood survey back soon … to get South Dakota pheasant management out of the dark ages.”

The harvest numbers Fredrickson refers to — which this story necessarily relies on, since it’s the only available data on recent pheasant hunting participation and harvest — elicit limited participation from residents (25 percent responded to the survey) and nonresidents (31 percent responded), among other issues.

Given that the state has discontinued those roadside brood counts, Johnson says South Dakota would rather nonresidents not know when it’s a bad pheasant year.

“Just believe us when we tell you, ‘Oh, pheasant hunting’s gonna be great,’” Johnson jokes. “But by doing that, we lose the scientific analysis on what really is happening with our bird population.”

It’s also unfair to traveling hunters, says 73-year-old resident duck and pheasant hunter Bruce Knowlan. No matter how much money they inject into the economy, most DIY pheasant hunters still have finite time and budgets to hunt birds.

“Hunting isn’t a sport anymore, it’s an industry,” says Knowlan of the canceled roadside counts and continued marketing during down bird years. “It’s crazy, it breaks my heart.”

None of the locals I spoke with for this story felt animosity toward well-mannered visiting hunters. That includes Knowlan, who lives in northeast South Dakota. The rural community he calls home is very welcoming to nonresident pheasant hunters.

“They like people who are spending money in the towns. They like nonresident money. They like their motels busy, they like their restaurants busy,” says Knowlan. “The only thing people around here hate is nonresidents coming into the area, buying farmland, and taking it out of [crop] production to put it into some program like CREP or CRP.”

In other words, farmers and residents are happy to have out-of-staters hunt pheasants, support local businesses, and maintain the area’s farming traditions and economy.

And indeed, nonresidents spent an estimated $161.8 million statewide during the 2025 season. While visiting hunters presumably spend more on expenses like lodging during pheasant season, it’s clear: resident pheasant hunters help drive the economy, too. Last year they spent an estimated $142.7 million in the state.

To Knowlan’s mind, there are fewer resident pheasant hunters in recent decades because there are simply fewer South Dakotans living in rural areas. Even as South Dakota’s population has grown steadily in the last few decades, most of that growth has gone into urban areas.

Case in point: The population of the northeast South Dakota county where Knowlan lives has steadily declined over the past century, dropping 64 percent to roughly 5,500 people in 2025. Meanwhile, farms have been consolidated, which means there are fewer landowners to ask for permission to hunt the same amount of ground.

“It’s fascinating to me that if you take the back roads from my place into town, there used to be a farm every 80 acres,” says Knowlan, adding that families used to have more kids, too. “I would go by a hundred kids’ homes in a six-mile radius. And now, on that same road, there’s maybe four. They’re bigger farms.”

Consolidation of small farms and the rise of hunting leases and access fees are key reasons Knowlan says it’s “much, much harder” to get permission on private land than it was decades ago.

“Residents aren’t going to go out and lease the neighbor’s land so they can go pheasant hunting,” says Knowlan. “It’s still a family tradition around here, but not like it used to be, where everybody would quit working at noon on Saturday and go pheasant hunting at the neighbor’s.”

Still, he doesn’t think South Dakota is different from any other state when it comes to diminished private access. Before he bought his South Dakota farm in 2011, Knowlan was a DIY hunter from Minnesota who hunted all over North America — including South Dakota — each season. He remembers what it was like to ask for permission, and what it’s like to grant it.

“I do think a lot of guys still would allow pheasant hunting, if they don’t have relatives or family members they’re saving it for,” says Knowlan. “It used to be, I’d say, 90 percent of people would let you hunt. Maybe now, around here, it’d be 50 percent. But I would hate to be a young man, 16 to 20 years old [again], and not have a lot of money and want to do it yourself all over the country. It would almost be impossible.”

Yes, there are more nonresident pheasant hunters than South Dakota pheasant hunters, and that means they’re killing more total pheasants. But locals are enjoying longer hunting seasons and they’re killing, on average, more birds per hunter than visitors more often than not in recent years.

Last season nonresidents, who are more likely to shell out for private access, killed an average of 9.3 pheasants. Residents killed an average of 10.2 birds during their season, and most probably didn’t pay for the privilege.

For an even greater edge, Johnson would like to expand the special three-day resident-only pheasant opener that falls on the weekend before nonresidents are allowed to hunt.

“People feel that expanding that might be helpful for giving in-state residents a little bit more of an advantage.”

Resident youth license sales are also on the rise in recent years. South Dakota already has strong opportunities for new hunters — adults and kids alike — to get into pheasant hunting.

Youth and mentored hunters “have a 9-day period to themselves for pheasant hunting” prior to the regular season opening,” according to SDGFP regs. “This is an opportunity for adults to take young hunters out to experience, first-hand, the joy of hunting as well as the responsibility.”

Still, there’s at least one complicating factor to recruiting younger hunters into the fold: You must be 16 to hunt alone in South Dakota. Adjusting this age restriction for qualified teens might be another way to encourage participation.

I learned of this requirement just a few weeks ago, after agreeing to take a local 15-year-old and his retriever pheasant hunting. I assumed they were still learning the ropes and needed guidance on strategy and an extra gun for the dog. This was true enough, but the reality was he needed me to legally chase pheasants. This South Dakota teenager (whose parents don’t hunt) needed an adult alongside him to shoot pheasants. The irony is that he drove himself to our meet up. You can start driving solo at 14 and a half in South Dakota.

South Dakota has some of the best public-land access around, especially compared to other historic pheasant states like Iowa and Nebraska. There are 1.6 million acres open to public hunting across South Dakota, according to SDGFP. And indeed, I have never once thought to ask strangers for pheasant hunting permission in seven years of bird hunting here, because of the abundance of uncrowded public land. The number of private acres enrolled in public-access programs has also grown over the last decade.

Still, Johnson would like to see South Dakota invest in drivable access to its public lands. (This echoes a larger national debate around what constitutes “access” to public lands.)

“South Dakota’s got a tremendous amount of public land. I think there are things that Game, Fish and Parks can do in managing that land that makes it easier, especially for older hunters, to get on it,” says Johnson. “We might have miles of public land in one tract and the parking lot is out by the road. And it’s fenced off. And so if you wanna get access deeper into the land, you’ve got to be in good physical shape to be able to walk deeper into it.”

SDGFP communications manager Nick Harrington says the agency absolutely cares about resident pheasant hunters and its goal is to recruit, retain, and reactivate as many pheasant hunters as possible. He’s attending a women’s learn-to-pheasant-hunt event Sunday, and also cited the grand opening of the agency’s expanded outdoor campus in Sioux Falls on Thursday.

“This expansion project is important as GFP will be able to offer more programs year-round,” reports KELO-AM, “which will include archery, game processing, and classes taking students on their hunting journey from the field to the table.”

I have volunteered as an instructor at a women’s learn-to-pheasant hunt weekend in South Dakota every August for the last four years. I’ve offered sessions on everything from shotgun basics to bird dog demos to finding promising public hunting spots. These programs, and my hunt with that local teen and other new pheasant hunters I’ve taken out, reminded me of what I’ve learned from years spent reporting on the national hunter recruitment problem. New adult hunters are usually recruited into the sport only after two to three years of continuous mentorship and support from dedicated existing hunters. Youth programs typically only succeed at recruiting kids who are already from hunting families.

The best way to keep family hunting traditions alive, in other words, is to take family hunting — again and again.

There is one simple correlation around pheasant hunting that’s difficult to ignore: Both resident and nonresident pheasant hunter numbers are linked to wild pheasant numbers. When harsh weather leads to low bird numbers, fewer hunters go pheasant hunting. When winter snowfall and spring rains are mild, bird numbers shoot up, and more hunters buy licenses. This is true in recent years, which have seen excellent bird harvests.

The most dedicated pheasant hunters will keep buying licenses every year, no matter how poor or good the hunting. It’s a tradition, and the best ones, including South Dakota pheasant hunting, will endure as long as there are places to hunt and people there who welcome hunters.

On my way out of my public spot near the hard-working combines, I waved to the young farmer still idling in the grain truck. He stepped out of the cab with a broad smile on his face and, dangling on the step, hollered over the rumble of harvesters.

“I can’t afford to repair your truck!” I shouted back.

He shook his head at that and, still grinning, waved me on.

Natalie Krebs is the Executive Editor of Outdoor Life, where she tackles everything from reporting digital features to producing podcast episodes. Originally from Missouri, she currently lives in northwest Arkansas with her bird dog, Hatchet.
