Delta Air Lines CEO Vows To Fight Heathrow Move That Separates It From Virgin Atlantic

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Delta Air Lines is fighting Heathrow Airport’s decision to move it away from Virgin Atlantic, warning that separating the partners will hurt passengers and undermine years of investment. I understand why Delta is upset. But whether that should entitle it to remain in Terminal 3 is a more complicated question.

Heathrow plans to move Delta from Terminal 3 to Terminal 4 in 2028, while Virgin Atlantic remains in Terminal 3. The decision would split an operation the two airlines have spent years integrating.

Delta CEO Ed Bastian issued an unusually pointed public statement on November 2, calling the decision “short-sighted, deeply disappointing and bad for airline consumers.” He argued that sharing Terminal 3 supports convenient connections and a consistent premium experience. Delta intends to challenge the move, saying it “will pursue all available remedies to correct this decision.”

“Heathrow Airport Limited’s decision to relocate Delta’s operation to Terminal 4, away from our partner Virgin Atlantic, is short-sighted, deeply disappointing and bad for airline consumers. For more than a decade, our airlines have served millions of customers through our co-located operation in Terminal 3, offering travelers convenient connections, premium amenities and access to an expanded global network.

“Delta and Virgin Atlantic provide UK consumers with a valuable competitive alternative across the Atlantic. Separating our operation undermines years of investment and disregards the tightly integrated customer benefits that we offer in our co-located terminal. We are committed to preserving the seamless travel experience for which Delta and Virgin Atlantic are known and will pursue all available remedies to correct this decision. We urge Heathrow Airport Limited to reconsider their decision in favor of a more prudent and thoughtful outcome that puts travelers first and allows competition to flourish at Heathrow.”

Terminal assignments can sound like an obscure airport planning issue until you are the passenger trying to make a connection.

A traveler arriving on Delta and continuing on Virgin Atlantic would face a terminal transfer that is unnecessary today. The same applies in reverse. Unfortunately, terminal transfers are not easy at LHR and moving between terminals introduces another step and more opportunity for something to go wrong.

For someone simply flying Delta to London and heading into the city, the effect may be less consequential. But Delta and Virgin sell their partnership as an integrated travel experience. Separating their airport operations makes that promise harder to deliver.

There is also the premium experience. Eligible Delta passengers currently benefit from Virgin Atlantic’s Terminal 3 facilities, including its Clubhouse. Those facilities are part of what makes the partnership attractive, especially when choosing between similarly priced business class tickets.

If the move happens, I would assume that a Delta flight departing from Terminal 4 will not include access to Virgin’s Terminal 3 facilities, at least in any practical capacity. However as I mentioned in my earlier story, integrating with Air France/KLM in Terminal 4 makes a lot of sense (far more connecting itineraries) and the lounge is not bad at all.

Still, replacing something that already works well requires money and effort. I can understand why Delta would prefer not to start over.

Heathrow’s decision follows a three-year terminal review. The airport says the changes will improve its use of existing capacity and operational resilience, while creating room for growth.

Heathrow also says airlines representing 81% of its passengers support the outcome (that is a share of passenger traffic represented by supportive airlines, not a survey showing that 81% of travelers approve).

Delta is not the only carrier being asked to move. The wider reshuffle also affects Finnair, Japan Airlines, LATAM, and China Southern.

An airport cannot necessarily give every airline its preferred location. Keeping one partnership together may mean imposing constraints or additional costs elsewhere. Delta’s investment deserves consideration, but it should not automatically settle how Heathrow should allocate scarce terminal space.

Bastian also presents the shared terminal as supporting competition across the Atlantic. I accept that a more cumbersome ground experience could make Delta and Virgin less attractive to some travelers. A passenger comparing connections may reasonably prefer an itinerary that avoids a terminal transfer.

But claims that the move will weaken competition or drive up fares need more support than the fact that Delta dislikes it. Heathrow is changing where Delta operates, rather than announcing that it must stop serving London or taking slots away. Delta’s strongest argument is the concrete one: it has built a useful shared operation with Virgin, and Heathrow is proposing to make that operation more difficult. That deserves a substantive answer but there’s undeniable logic in the shuffling.

Delta has good reason to challenge a Heathrow move that would separate it from Virgin Atlantic. Sharing Terminal 3 offers practical benefits, and I understand Bastian’s anger. But Heathrow must manage the airport as a whole. I would be more persuaded by a clear account of what this reshuffle improves and why those gains outweigh the disruption.

For now, this is a dispute over a planned 2028 change..itt does not mean your next Delta flight has moved to Terminal 4. I hope the eventual outcome preserves as much of the current passenger experience as possible, but I’m still not convinced Delta is being unfairly picked on by LHR and I am convinced that whether Delta wins or loses this battle, its passengers will enjoy a great ground product at Heathrow for many years to come.

Join our mailing list for a daily summary of posts! We never sell your info.

Matthew is an avid traveler who calls Los Angeles home. Each year he travels more than 200,000 miles by air and has visited more than 135 countries. Working both in the aviation industry and as a travel consultant, Matthew has been featured in major media outlets around the world and uses his Live and Let's Fly blog to share the latest news in the airline industry, commentary on frequent flyer programs, and detailed reports of his worldwide travel.

Not to defend Delta, but why would anyone connect from DL onto AF/KL at LHR. It just adds complextity and cost versus flying DL to AMS/CDG and connecting from there. I doubt they have anything to gain by being co-located with AF/KL rather than VS. Also T4 is worse than the T2/T3 complex for ground transport or self-connecting options.

Is terminal 4 underutilized or are they shifting the problem of crowding from T3 to T4? With LHR ruling out the prospect of new slots at LHR in 2028 when Heathrow’s Management says this move will lead to gains the question they haven’t answered is gains for who, for which airline.

If this move is really about alleviating congestion in T3 then go for it. But if this move is part of a broader plan that will see Delta capacity at T3 replaced by another carrier then Delta has every right to throw a fit.

T4 is operating significantly under capacity and since BA needs to move additional capacity into T3 to relieve T5, it has to be one of the larger T3 tenants who moves out.

Delta doesn’t adult very well and tends to react particularly poorly when told no. Not everyone is going to come out a winner and they just need to deal with the fact that this is a loss for them.

A better solution would be to move all SkyTeam out of Terminal 3 into Terminal 4, move all OneWorld and its partners into Terminals 5 and 3 and distribute the non-aligned between Terminals 2 and 3 as space allows. Terminal 4 is much more modern than Terminal 3 so there should be little complaint on that one.

I tend to agree and the current two-level Qatar lounge would make a great new Clubhouse. LHR should do this and just help VS/DL pay for it…

Yep. This was the obvious move by LHR if that’s your end game. End of the day this will be about compensating DL/VS for the move to T4. But VS(/DL) gets no brownie points for renovating the current Clubhouse in a transparent attempt to forestall this. LHR told VS/DL that this was the likely outcome – if VS(/DL) wanted to waste their money playing a game of chicken, that’s on them.

Are there really that many people connecting in LHR to Asia on Virgin?

While the clubhouse is nice I wasn’t overly wowed by it. And since flights are day time west, it’s not as important having a lounge meal like eastbound when you’re trying to maximize sleep. The Air France lounge in T4 looks nice enough anyways. I think the biggest loss for anyone flying D1 is the arrivals lounge, especially if you’re heading straight into the office.

Maybe Delta should have listened to their pilots a few years ago and stopped outsourcing so much of their international flying to partners…

” Delta’s strongest argument is the concrete one: it has built a useful shared operation with Virgin, and Heathrow is proposing to make that operation more difficult.”

Heathrow is proposing to level the playing field between the two major US-UK Joint Ventures at the airport. Virgin had the chance to move, as well. It’s not as though Delta doesn’t have the cash to build a reasonable ground experience at T4. LHR isn’t even anything close to a transfer hub for DL. Those hubs are AMS and CDG — CDG, an airport where DL/AF customers have to change terminals and clear security between terminals. what a concept for Delta.

I get why Delta is upset but it doesn’t seem very persuasive to be mad about “connections” when LHR isn’t much of a connecting airport for their Joint Venture. But as usual… Delta and Virgin Atlanta love to cry foul when asked to do what AA/BA do today…

20 Ways to Keep Warm Without Turning on the Heat

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 4, 2026 1:38 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Tips, tricks & ideas for a better home and yard, delivered to your inbox daily.

When winter winds blow, some homeowners dread the accompanying—and often outrageous— heating bills that Jack Frost brings with him. Heating oil prices for 2026, which are topping $6 a gallon, are particularly painful; overall, according to the National Energy Assistance Directors Association (NEADA), homeowners can expect to pay an average of 8.7 percent more to heat their homes than they did last winter.

While you probably won’t get away with not turning on the heat at all this winter, these smart solutions can help minimize winter’s impact on your budget.

Drafty windows are some of the biggest culprits when it comes to heat loss, which is why it’s important to insulate them properly. For an inexpensive fix, cut a piece of bubble wrap to fit perfectly inside the window frame, spray a thin film of water on the window, and press the bubble wrap into the glass. The layer of plastic will let the sunlight in while keeping the warmth where it belongs: inside.

The 3M window insulation kit is 0.75 mils thick and comes in one cut-to-fit sheet that measures 17.5 feet long by 5.16 feet wide.   Read the review.

Warm air rises, which isn’t great news for our heating bills. Although it might seem as though switching your ceiling fan on would make your room colder rather than warmer, turning on the fan can actually make a room toastier so long as you set the blades to spin in a clockwise manner.

To reverse the direction of your ceiling fan, check for a switch at the base of your fan. This change of direction creates a slight updraft that will push the warm air toward the walls and back down into the room.

Air leaks are a major source of heat loss. Check your home for possible gaps with this easy test: Hold a piece of toilet paper in front of your window and door frames. If the toilet paper moves, you’ve found a crack that needs to be sealed. Use weatherstripping to fill the gaps so that the heat stays tucked inside, and consider adding foam insulation to outlets to reduce heat loss even more. Caulk, too, is a useful weapon against leaks. “Use caulk for stationary cracks and gaps, especially around window frames or where siding meets the home’s foundation,” says HVAC technician Josh Mitchell, who works with AirConditionerLab in Nevada City, Calif.

RELATED: The 20 Best Products for Surviving Winter

If you have radiators in your home, try fashioning reflective plates out of aluminum foil and placing one behind each unit. Instead of just heating up your wall, the radiator will heat the foil, which will reflect warmth back into the room and allow you to maximize the unit’s output.

Exhaust fans are effective at ridding your bathroom of humid air during and after a hot shower. But leaving the fan off altogether when you shower is a good strategy for room heat generation, one that works particularly well if you leave the bathroom door open while you shower. The steam warms the air in the bathroom, and in adjacent halls and rooms.

If you have drafty doors, craft an inexpensive DIY fix from foam pipe insulation. Cut the foam to size, and wrap it around the bottom of your doors—yes, even the interior ones!—to prevent heat from escaping and utility costs from going up. If you’re not up for a project, there are plenty of affordable draft dodgers you can purchase. Draft dodgers are easy to slide off the door when the weather warms up, so you have no excuse not to try this budget-saving solution.

If you don’t have a jacket for your water heater, odds are you’re letting its warmth go to waste. Consider insulating your unit to reduce heat loss and save a little cash—about $20 a month, according to the U.S. Department of Energy. To see if your water heater needs additional insulation, just touch it. If it feels warm, it could probably use a water heater blanket, but check your owner’s manual first to make sure it’s safe to cover your model.

RELATED: Solved! When to Replace a Water Heater, Explained

This is by far the easiest thing you can do to lower home heating bills: Raise the blinds and open the curtains when the sun is out. It’s especially important to keep window coverings open on south-facing windows, because they bring in the warmest afternoon sun. Just remember to close your curtains again at night to keep all the toasty air inside until the sun returns.

Consider replacing your thermostat with a smart thermostat, which “allows you to set different temperature schedules throughout the day based on your occupancy patterns,” according to Justin Bohannon of Affordable Solutions HVAC & Electrical in Durham, North Carolina. “By utilizing setback temperatures during times of lower demand, such as when you’re at work or sleeping, you can save energy and reduce heating costs,” he adds.

Sensi Touch 2 Smart Thermostat is for those who want a clean interface, dependable performance, and thoughtful privacy protections. Read the review.

Iced coffee or iced tea may be your go-to beverage to help you get through the day, but come winter it might be time to make the switch to a warmer beverage. Hot cocoa, tea, and coffee will warm you up on the inside and still give you that kick of caffeine you need.

A top sheet and comforter might be plenty comfortable from spring to fall, but in winter, it’s time to burrow under multiple layers. Try making your bed with flannel sheets and fleece or electric blankets, then drop your thermostat down a couple of degrees at nighttime to stay warm without burning through extra cash.

Cozying up next to a roaring fire is a way to warm up that comes with a caveat. In traditional fireplaces, about 80 percent of the heat generated escapes up the chimney. In fact, an open fireplace can even suck warm air out of the room. So while you do get some warmth from a fire in the fireplace, it may be less than you might think. To maximize your fireplace’s heat output, consider upgrading it with energy-efficient, wood-burning inserts.

If you do light a fire in the fireplace, remember to close the flue after you have put the fire out (keep the flue open, of course, while the fire is burning). Otherwise, all the warm air will go up and out of the house and let in the frigid air from outside.

RELATED: 11 Mistakes You Should Never Make With Your Fireplace

Layering clothing can goes a long way toward keeping you warm. While you’re at home, wear long sleeves, and wear socks (maybe even heated socks) and slippers around the house. Fashion retailer Uniqlo sells shirts, undershirts, long underwear, leggings, and socks with Heattech technology that traps heat to keep you warm. When you can’t shake a chill, throw your sweatshirt in the dryer for a quick spin to warm it up.

Check all your air vents to make sure they aren’t covered by window treatments or furniture. “Rearranging furniture is a surprisingly effective way to reduce home heating bills,” says Guy Sharp, a relocation manager at Andorra Guides who help residents settle into new homes, with a special focus on staying warm on a budget in the chilly temperatures that characterize the small European country nestled in the Pyrenees mountains.

“When you position your furniture away from radiators, baseboard heaters, and vents, you allow the heat to circulate freely throughout the room,” he adds. Blocked vents also makes your furnace work harder than it should and increases air pressure in the ductwork, which can lead to cracks. If it’s not possible to rearrange your furniture, you can use vent extenders to direct air around obstructions.

If you have an attic, it’s a good idea to confirm that there’s sufficient insulation up there and that the insulation is properly affixed. Look for gaps around pipes and ducts, and make sure that there aren’t any loose bits. Add more attic insulation if you find that what’s there could use some shoring up.

If your floors feel cold, throwing down some area rugs can will warm your feet up in a jif. Rugs offer a barrier against chilly air rising up from your concrete foundation, or from the crawl space under your home. For even more insulation (and to keep the rugs from slipping), use rug pads under your rugs.

If your home has seldom-used rooms, close them off. There’s no sense in heating the guest room, for example, if you’re not expecting visitors for a while. “Also make sure that you close any vents in little-used rooms,” says Leonard Ang, CEO of iPropertyManagement, an online company that offers advice for landlords, investors, and tenants throughout the United States.

For a thorough evaluation of how to best protect your home against winter’s chill, consider scheduling an energy assessment or energy audit. In such an audit, professionals use special equipment that draws in air through any gaps in your home’s exterior, allowing them to pinpoint areas that should be sealed or insulated.

When you do eventually turn on the heat, make sure your furnace is ready for a season of use. Having your furnace serviced in the fall is an often overlooked step that can help you save money on winter heating costs. “Regular maintenance of your heating system ensures it operates at peak efficiency,” says Brad Roberson, president of Aire Serv, an HVAC service company that has locations around the country.

Old Houses AREN’T Scary: What We’ve Learned and Loved About Them

From crown molding and built-ins to creaky floors and drafty windows, old houses are full of charm and challenges. So we’re celebrating all the things that make old homes worth loving, living in, and fixing up. Learn about historic house styles; find practical advice for maintaining your old home; and discover quirky, forgotten features you simply won’t find in new builds.

Unlock Your Full Potential by Fixing These Common Triathlon Body Imbalances

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Triathletes can face muscle imbalances. These off-season exercises help build proper strength to prepare for next season. (Photo: Brad Kaminski/Triathlete)

Triathlon training is all about repetition, with hours upon hours spent catching and pulling in the pool, turning the pedals on the bike, and turning the legs over on the run. With that repetition comes natural imbalance, with some muscle groups getting overworked while others get neglected (or even weaken with age).

Over time, these imbalances can impact performance directly or indirectly by contributing to injury development. That’s why taking time to identify and address these imbalances now will help keep you healthy in the long run.

By pinpointing areas of weakness or asymmetry early on, athletes can target the stabilizing muscles, improve biomechanical efficiency across all three disciplines, and significantly reduce the risk of overuse injuries that often arise from repetitive strain. Here’s how.

While swimming is generally thought of as the “safest” discipline in triathlon, issues can and do occur, often manifesting as “swimmer’s shoulder.” Swimmer’s shoulder is a bit of a catch-all term for pain in the front of the shoulder that encompasses several pathologies, including rotator cuff issues.

Typically, underlying laxity and instability exist. One factor that can contribute to shoulder problems is a strength imbalance between the shoulder internal and external rotators, with the internal rotators becoming disproportionately strong in comparison to the external rotators. This makes sense, as the shoulder internal rotators are active throughout the catch and pull, working to rotate the arm in and push against water.

But the external rotators still play an important role, as they help to stabilize the shoulder joint, keeping the ball of the joint centered in the socket to help prevent impingement and pain with overhead use and shoulder abnormalities. To decrease injury risk, external rotators should have about two-thirds of the strength of the internal rotators.

The most reliable, valid ways to check this involve specialized dynamometry equipment that measures force. But if you’re just looking to get a general idea, check how many reps of each of the below motions you can do until fatigue.

In this case, the test (lying prone, rotating the back of the arm up) can also be used as an effective strengthening exercise for external rotation. Start with higher reps, lower weights to learn the motion (3 sets of 10-12), then work up to higher weights, lower reps (4 sets of 6-8) to build more strength.

If this position causes pain or is too difficult at first, a side-lying shoulder external rotation is also a good option.

While the incidence of overuse injuries attributed to cycling is generally lower than those attributed to running, they can and do occur, most commonly impacting the knee and lower back. Although singling out contributing factors is difficult, the prolonged posture and muscle activations associated with cycling predispose athletes to overdeveloped quadriceps and an undeveloped posterior chain (glutes, hamstrings, back extensors) and lateral hip muscles.

Cyclists need strong quads – these are the predominant muscle used to power the pedal stroke. But the glutes and hamstrings do assist, so neglecting those leaves some power on the table. Plus, that injury-prone lower back needs adequate strength and endurance to withstand hours spent in a forward-flexed position.

Also, because muscle weakness and tightness often go hand-in-hand, related limitations in mobility throughout the posterior chain limit a cyclist’s ability to comfortably hold more aggressive aero positions. Those quads of steel will only help so much if you’re sitting up to ease back and glute discomfort by the end of a long ride!

The strength and endurance of the posterior chain (and also the lateral hip, which can play a role when it comes to knee pain on the bike) can be checked using a single-leg bridge hold.

The single-leg RDL is a good choice for eliciting both high force and stretch in the hamstrings and strengthening the gluteal muscles, back extensors, and lateral hips. Isolating each leg can also help with side-to-side imbalances.

When it comes to muscle strength and running, the glutes have been frequently implicated as the cause of (and solution to) many issues. But, while the glutes do play an important stability-related role in the run gait and deficits have been linked with injury, the true workhorse of the run gait – the calves – often gets overlooked and under-strengthened.

The soleus (one of the calf muscles) experiences forces of up to eight times body weight and produces up to 50% of forward propulsive power while running. Because the calves insert onto the heel via the Achilles tendon (which acts as a spring during running), the two are linked, with decreased calf strength seen in athletes with Achilles issues compared to healthy controls.

Strong calves can also help to protect against tibial bone stress injuries. Masters athletes lose calf strength (and Achilles tendon stiffness) disproportionately to other muscle groups with age, leading to both a reduction in propulsion and step length and an increased risk of lower leg soft tissue injuries such as strains and tendinopathies. So, when it comes to both performance and helping to guard against many common lower leg injuries, first check the powerhouse muscles below the knee!

Calf strength can be tested with a single-leg calf raise test.

The test here can again be used as the exercise – just load it up!

So, while the different disciplines and repeated motions of triathlon might lend athletes to certain imbalances, not all is lost! Triathletes can make themselves stronger, more resilient, and faster with some awareness and work – so don’t neglect those details.

One of Nintendo's Switch and Switch 2 cartridge manufacturers is exiting the business, which could make physical games more expensive

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Macronix, longtime Nintendo hardware partner and one of the main producers of ROM memory, has announced it's leaving the business. ROM chips are key components in Switch and Switch 2 cartridges.

Industry analyst David Gibson shared the news on X, which Macronix announced in its latest earnings call. The company plans to phase out ROM products entirely by the end of the third quarter of fiscal year 2026.

As it stands, ROM products represent just 5 percent of Macronix's business, going off its reported sales for the third quarter. This is down significantly from 26 percent just one year ago. As a result, Macronix is shifting manufacturing capacity to instead focus on NOR (flash) and eMMC memory chips.

It's worth noting that Macronix is not the only company Nintendo relies on for ROM memory. Nintendo also sources parts from Rohm Co. (Oki Semiconductor), but losing a key partner puts more pressure on the already strained supply, which could result in higher prices.

As Gibson pointed out, that could also push more publishers - including Nintendo - to ship their games as Game-Key Cards instead, which do not include physical cartridges, avoiding the extra cost.

Although not directly stated, this is likely another side effect of the ongoing, global AI-driven memory and storage crisis. On top of making many consumer products unaffordable for millions, causing the prices of game consoles to rise and putting GPUs out of reach for PC builders, the shortages have also caused major memory manufacturers to redirect their resources to business segments where demand is higher.

Nearly one year ago, Micron, one of the three largest memory manufacturers in the world, announced it's exiting the consumer memory business and shutting down its Crucial brand.

This could put more pressure on Nintendo, already one of the last holdouts continuing to invest in physical media, to raise prices of games even further or abandon cartridges. Just last month, Nintendo said it has no plans to ditch physical games, in the wake of rival Sony's decision to end disc manufacturing in 2028.

A Virtual Lifeless Earth Will Help Scientists Find Life on Exoplanets

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

In the search for life elsewhere in the galaxy, astronomers sift through exoplanet discoveries looking for ones that could be habitable. That means rocky planets with atmospheres in habitable zones. But one thing stymies these efforts. Can we examine atmospheres and differentiate between an exoplanet that's habitable and one that's actually inhabited?

That's difficult, partly because exoplanets are so far away, and also because we only have Earth to guide us. It's the only habitable and inhabited world we know of. But new research shows how we can work with those facts to try to detect inhabited worlds.

The work is "A Whole-planet Model of the Earth without Life for Terrestrial Exoplanet Studies," and it's published in The Planetary Science Journal. The lead author is Samantha Gilbert-Janizek, who was a postdoctoral astronomy student at the University of Washington when conducting this work.

"As the only known habitable (and inhabited) planet in the Universe, Earth informs our search for life elsewhere," the authors write. "Future telescopes like the Habitable Worlds Observatory (HWO) will soon look for life on rocky worlds around Sun-like stars, so it is critical that we understand how to distinguish habitable planets from inhabited planets."

Observatories like the Habitable Worlds Observatory (HWO) will inject new energy into the search for life. They will shift exoplanet science from identifying worlds that could be habitable, to identifying worlds with atmospheres showing signs of life. But as the researchers point out, we still don't know how to tell inhabited worlds, if there are any, from those that are merely habitable.

"Earth is currently the only known inhabited planet in the Universe, and geological evidence suggests that it was habitable with liquid-water oceans for most of its 4.54 Gyr history," the authors write. "Owing to its long-term habitability, Earth serves as the template that guides our search for biospheres elsewhere."

The researchers tackled this issue by building a sort of virtual Earth, but one without life.

"As a first step toward answering these questions, we present a coupled interior-atmosphere evolution model of the Earth without life from 50 Myr to 5 Gyr that reproduces 19 key observations of the preindustrial Earth after 4.5 Gyr within estimated measurement uncertainties," the researchers explain. Those 19 observations included atmospheric CO2 and H2O, surface temperature and albedo, ocean mass, and even factors like upper-mantle temperature and upper-mantle heat flow. These non-biological factors all shape a planet's atmosphere and have to be accounted for.

"Though exoplanet interiors themselves will likely never be directly observable, the effects of interior evolution are apparent in the composition of the atmosphere and the albedo of the surface environment, both of which HWO will observe on Earth-like planets around Sun-like stars via reflected-light spectroscopy," the authors explain.

This schematic shows some of the key processes included in the abiotic Earth model. Remotely observable quantities (surface temperature, albedo, and atmospheric H2O and CO2) are in bold. Image Credit: Samantha Gilbert-Janizek et al 2026 Planet. Sci. J. 7 216

The researchers also produced reflected light spectra of the model Earth that covers the likely wavelength range of the HWO. They used a code to compute a "synthetic HWO reflected-light spectra (0.2â2.0 Î¼m) for the true Earth and the abiotic Earthâs atmospheric state after 4.5 Gyr," the authors write. These included line absorptions for CO2, H2O, O2, O3, and CH4.

This virtual Earth without life can serve as a benchmark to compare with detailed atmosphere observations of habitable worlds.

âThis model can help us see how habitable but non-living planets might mimic living planets,â lead author Gilbert-Janizek said. "When we interpret observations of an exoplanetâs atmosphere, this tool will help us determine the probability that what we observe indicates life on the surface.â

This schematic shows the atmospheric sources and sinks that define the new volatile cycling processes in the model. Fluxes related to CO2 are color-coded magenta, and fluxes related to H2O are color-coded teal. The schematic doesn't include ocean chemistry. Image Credit: Samantha Gilbert-Janizek et al 2026 Planet. Sci. J. 7 216

Gilbert-Janizek didn't start from scratch. Senior author Rory Barnes, a UW associate professor of astronomy, had already built a model that included the core, mantle, crust and stellar radiation. This work added the surface and the atmosphere to build a more comprehensive model.

The authors hope that this model can serve as a kind of plug-and-play model that can be adjusted to fit with any particular exoplanet being studied. They can input factors like albedo and atmospheric CO2 and test what happens. If the model then recreates the observations of the planet, it suggests that the exoplanet in question is uninhabited.

Naturally, an undertaking of this size and complexity requires some assumptions. The model initializes after Earth's magma ocean solidified, and that dictated the atmosphere's initial composition. The authors say they chose an initial set of conditions "that apply after the bulk of the mantle has solidified."

They also assumed that plate tectonics were active during all of Earth's history beginning in the middle Hadean, which is widely believed to be true and in line with evidence. There are alternative hypotheses saying that this wasn't the case. One says that plate tectonics only began when it shifted from a stagnant-lid to modern plate tectonics during the Archaean, but the researchers avoided that.

Other assumptions were made regarding atmospheric pressure, the strength of Earth's geodynamo, and even atmospheric escape of hydrogen. But these types of assumptions are a normal part of science and are explained up front.

But the virtual Earth model successfully reproduces the properties of the Pre-Industrial Earth, and that's the most important thing. It also predicts that a planet can maintain habitable conditions for a long time without requiring the presence of life. This is relevant because false positives are one of the HWO's challenges.

These are three Engineering Architecture Concepts (EACs) for the Habitable Worlds Observatory. Each one features a different mirror design. EAC-1 is a 6m, segmented, off-axis telescope. EAC-2 is the same but uses a round keystone mirror and surrounding segments. EAC-3 is an 8m on-axis telescope with round segments. Not shown is the extendable, protective barrel that each design has, which will protect the mirror from micrometeorites. Image Credit: NASA Goddard/Conceptual Image Labs

âDr. Gilbert-Janizekâs work represents an exciting step toward understanding where life can exist in the universe,â Barnes said. âAfter more than a decade of work by many scientists, Dr. Gilbert-Janizek has finally completed a model of the Earth that simultaneously reproduces properties of Earthâs core, mantle, surface and atmosphere. That calibration gives us confidence that future simulations of exoplanets will be accurate.â

The question "Are we alone in the Universe" can be asked in one breath but not answered in one. It has to be broken down to much smaller questions asked and answered across many generations. Even "Are we alone in the Milky Way" is far too large and encompassing a question. "Are we alone" has to be asked exoplanet by exoplanet, and that requires tools of the sort developed by Dr. Gilbert-Janizek and her colleagues.

Our first 6,000 exoplanet discoveries were bulk discoveries. But the search is becoming more targeted toward Earth-like planets, meaning tools like this one are becoming more and more necessary. The upcoming HWO, if it gets built, places this point in even finer focus.

âRealizing the ambitious goals of a telescope like the Habitable Worlds Observatory requires decades of work across many generations of scientists,â Gilbert-Janizek said. âIâm so grateful to join this lineage and one day pass the torch to the next generation so that we can continue chipping away at this question of whether or not we are alone in the universe.â

Energy Drinks, Stimulant Supplements Linked to Higher Heart Risks in Young Adults

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Your hub for heart health management, insights, and solutions

Learn the basics to keeping your heart healthy

New research suggests that caffeinated energy drinks and stimulant supplements are associated with significantly higher risks of serious heart problems, including cardiac arrest and heart attack in young adults.

The findings, to be presented at the American Heart Association (AHA) Scientific Sessions 2026 November 6–9 in Chicago, are preliminary and can’t prove that energy drinks cause heart problems.

Still, the results highlight concerning cardiovascular effects of these popular products, particularly among adults under 35 who consume them regularly.

“These products are marketed as everyday pick-me-ups and sold next to soda and bottled water, so most people assume they carry no real risk,” study author Sai Samayamanthula, a fourth-year medical student at the University of Virginia School of Medicine in Charlottesville, VA, said in a news release.

“For many healthy people, if these products are consumed occasionally, the risk is probably small. However, individuals who consume energy drinks or other stimulant-containing supplements regularly can quickly exceed the recommended daily limit for caffeine, so it’s important for people to know that there may be a risk of cardiovascular harm with these products,” Samayamanthula continued.

Researchers from the University of Virginia School of Medicine analyzed 28,345 reports submitted to the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) between 2017 and 2025.

Of these, 854 involved caffeinated energy drinks or stimulant supplements, while the remaining reports involved non-stimulant products.

Compared with non-stimulant products, cardiac arrest was reported approximately 11 times more often with energy drinks and stimulant supplements. Heart attacks were reported 4.5 times more often, while ventricular arrhythmias and palpitations were reported about four times more frequently.

Nearly 45% of heart-related reports involved adults ages 18 to 34.

“Our findings support the case for more systematic safety monitoring of caffeine- and stimulant-containing products. In addition, healthcare professionals should remember to ask their younger patients who have palpitations, chest pain or fainting spells if they recently or regularly consume these products,” Samayamanthula said.

Gregory M. Marcus, MD, professor of medicine at the University of California, San Francisco School of Medicine, and chair of the 2026 AHA scientific statement on caffeine and cardiovascular disease, wasn’t involved in the study, but said the research “raises concerns that products with very high amounts of caffeine may be harmful to the cardiovascular system.”

“Although there are biological reasons why that connection could be possible, the study does not prove that caffeine products cause these events, because other factors may have influenced the results found in this analysis,” Marcus said in a news release.

“More research is needed to understand whether these stimulant products are truly responsible for the reported heart-related events.”

Cheng-Han Chen, MD, board certified interventional cardiologist and medical director of the Structural Heart Program at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center in Laguna Hills, CA, said the findings, although concerning, are not surprising. Chen wasn’t involved in the study.

“We know that energy drinks can have significant effects on the cardiovascular system, primarily because of their stimulant properties,” he told Healthline.

“Caffeine can increase blood pressure and heart rate, while other ingredients, such as guarana and taurine, may further contribute to cardiovascular stimulation and increased stress hormone levels. What is particularly concerning is the potential for these products to trigger serious complications, including abnormal heart rhythms, especially in people with underlying cardiovascular conditions.”

Chen agreed that occasional energy drink consumption is unlikely to have long-term consequences for heart health in most healthy individuals. However, he said that “frequent or excessive consumption can potentially increase the risk of hypertension and cardiac arrhythmias.”

An AHA scientific statement published in July 2026 notes that up to 400 milligrams of caffeine daily is generally considered safe for most healthy adults. That’s roughly equivalent to four or five 8-ounce cups of brewed coffee, depending on how it’s prepared.

However, some energy drinks contain up to three times as much caffeine per fluid ounce as brewed coffee.

Certain energy drinks may also contain stimulants, such as guarana, yerba mate, theobromine, synephrine, and yohimbine.

These ingredients may compound caffeine’s effects on the cardiovascular system.

“Caffeine stimulates the cardiovascular system, primarily by increasing sympathetic nervous system activity. This can cause the heart to beat faster, raise blood pressure, and increase the workload on the heart,” Chen said.

“Energy drinks may contain additional stimulants, such as guarana and taurine, which can further increase blood pressure and stress hormone levels. With excessive consumption, these effects can become more pronounced and potentially interfere with the heart’s normal electrical activity, increasing the risk of abnormal heart rhythms. People with underlying cardiovascular conditions, particularly hypertension or a predisposition to arrhythmias, may be especially vulnerable to these effects.”

Other research has echoed these concerns. A 2025 review suggests that high-volume energy drink consumption can prolong the heart’s QTc interval, increase blood pressure, and raise heart rate beyond the effects of caffeine alone.

“There is growing concern about the cardiovascular effects of energy drinks and stimulant-containing supplements, particularly among younger adults,” Chen said.

“One challenge is that younger individuals may not recognize that these products can have significant effects on their heart and blood pressure, especially when consumed frequently or in combination with other caffeinated products.”

Young adults accounted for a substantial portion of the FDA reports included in the study, highlighting potential cardiovascular concerns for this age group.

People with existing heart conditions, a history of irregular heart rhythms, or heightened caffeine sensitivity may face additional risks. Those with undiagnosed genetic heart conditions may also be vulnerable.

A 2024 study identified cases of sudden cardiac arrest occurring shortly after energy drink consumption, particularly among people with underlying inherited heart conditions.

“As a cardiologist, I am particularly concerned about people who may have underlying cardiovascular conditions that have not yet been diagnosed,” Chen said.

“Excessive stimulant intake can potentially trigger symptoms such as palpitations, chest discomfort, and lightheadedness. In susceptible individuals, it may also increase the risk of more serious complications, including abnormal heart rhythms.”

Additionally, combining energy drinks with strenuous exercise or alcohol may introduce additional cardiovascular risks.

The findings suggest an association between energy drinks, stimulant supplements, and reported cardiovascular events, but they are preliminary and do not establish cause and effect.

The FDA database also relied on voluntary adverse-event reports, meaning some events may never be reported, while others may receive disproportionate attention.

Researchers also do not yet know how many people consumed these products without experiencing any problems. Without this variable, the reports cannot establish actual risk.

Other factors, including underlying health conditions, medications, and lifestyle habits, may also influence the results. More systematic research and controlled clinical trials are needed.

For most healthy adults, consuming up to 400 milligrams of caffeine per day is generally considered safe.

However, Chen noted that individual tolerance for caffeine may vary, and people with underlying cardiovascular conditions, particularly high blood pressure or abnormal heart rhythms, may need to limit or avoid caffeine altogether.

“Energy drinks can vary considerably in their caffeine content and serving sizes,” Chen said.

“Depending on the product, a single can may contain anywhere from 80 to 300 milligrams of caffeine. This means that consuming even one or two energy drinks can put someone close to or above the recommended daily limit, particularly when combined with coffee, tea, or other caffeinated supplements.”

Seek emergency medical attention if you experience chest pain, particularly if it’s accompanied by shortness of breath, fainting, or other symptoms of a possible heart attack.

If you live with a heart condition, ask your doctor about your daily caffeine intake to determine whether you need to cut back.

According to Chen, warning signs of excessive stimulant intake may include:

“Depending on the severity of these symptoms, medical attention may be necessary,” he said.

Still, most people may not need to give up caffeine entirely. Monitoring your habits could help protect your heart health. This may include:

“I encourage people to read product labels carefully and be aware of their total daily caffeine consumption,” Chen said. “I particularly recommend that people with underlying cardiovascular conditions avoid energy drinks.”

Locust Pose Might Be a Simple Backbend, But It Can Help Improve Your Posture

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

In Yoga Journal’s Archives series, we share a curated collection of articles originally published in past issues beginning in 1975. This article about the backbend Locust Pose first appeared in the July-August 1996 issue of Yoga Journal. Find more of our Archives here.

If a picture is worth a thousand words, then our posture speaks volumes. Poor carriage of the torso—rounded shoulders, sunken chest, and a downward cast of the eyes—generally suggests a negative state of being. Good posture, on the other hand—shoulders rolled back and down, chest lifted, and an energetic carriage—demonstrates confidence and a sense of purpose.

All the backbending yoga postures help create this physical expression of assurance. You feel better, have more energy, and benefit from an enhanced respiratory system. The internal alignment of the organs is refined as we adjust and improve the external alignment.

The postures actually have the potential to positively and profoundly affect you on all levels: physical, mental, emotional, and spiritual. Approaching your asanas with this awareness is the key to the enrichment of your practice.

The repertoire of backbending postures, or hyperextensions of the spine, is vast. In this article, we are going to focus on one of the more basic back-bending poses, Grasshopper or Locust Pose (Salabhasana). There are several variations of this posture, according to different systems or styles of hatha yoga. We will practice two versions of Locust, beginning with Salabhasana One as described in B.K.S. Iyengar’s Light on Yoga.

Locust is excellent for correcting poor posture, as well as for counteracting the development of kyphosis, the rounding of the upper back. It tones the whole posterior of the body, strengthens the abdominals, and enhances digestion.

Begin by lying face down on your mat, with your arms at your sides near your hips, palms facing upward. Tuck your chin in, forehead on the floor.

Engage your abdominals by drawing your navel in and up, to stabilize your lower back. Contract your buttocks and quadriceps. Keep the knees, legs, and feet together. Exhale the breath as you simultaneously raise the head, chest, arms, and legs. Look forward and up. Hold and breathe for five to 10 seconds. Keep the abdominals firm so you feel the support from underneath.

Exhale as you come out of the pose, returning to base position. Turn your head to the side and completely relax. Repeat once or twice more.

Full Locust begins in the same base position, but with the arms outstretched at shoulder level, perpendicular to the body. The palms are facing down, and the chin is on the mat. The knees, legs, and feet are together.

Engage your abdominal muscles by drawing your navel in and up; contract your buttocks and quadriceps. This time, inhale the breath as you raise the arms, chest, head, and legs. Look forward and up. Breathe naturally as you hold the posture for five to 10 seconds. Keep your shoulders away from your earlobes as you continue to stretch your arms back and slightly upward. Palms remain facing the floor. The idea is to lift the entire body upward as you balance on the center of your abdomen.

Exhale as you come out of the pose, returning to base position, and relax. Repeat once or twice more.

If your lower back is sensitive or you are prone to chronic neck tension, you may want to practice the pose in the following stages.

Lie on your stomach, face down, arms at your sides near your hips. Palms are facing up, the chin is tucked in, and the forehead is on the floor. Legs are hip-distance apart. Firm the buttocks and abdominals as well as the quadriceps.

Inhale the breath. Exhaling, raise just the arms to about hip level, keeping the palms facing up. The head remains on the floor. Lengthen the neck and focus on strengthening your back muscles. Feel your shoulder blades pull down your back and slightly together.

Keep the abdominals, buttocks, and legs firm. Exhale and lower down.

Next, repeat what you’ve just done and add the lifting of the chest. The neck and head will come up as well, but keep looking downward so the neck remains extended rather than arched. Inhale in your base position. Exhale and raise the arms only. Inhale and hold. Ex-haling, raise the chest. Again, hold and breathe rhythmically for five to 10 seconds. Exhale as you lower down.

In the same beginning position, inhale the breath, and exhale raising the arms up. Hold and inhale. Exhale, raising the chest. Hold and inhale. Finally, exhale and raise the legs. Hold and breathe. Keep looking at the floor, focusing on lengthening the body from the top of your head to your fingertips on down to your toes. When you’re ready, lower down on an exhalation. Turn your head to the side, release the pose completely, and relax.

A natural inclination after performing a back bend is to pull your knees to your chest. So after your last repetition, you may want to got into Child’s Pose: Kneel back onto your heels, folding your torso over your thighs with your head resting on the floor in front of your knees. Arms are at your sides with palms facing up.

If your head doesn’t rest on the floor easily, simply fold your arms underneath your forehead for support.

Fate of the Galaxy is a huge, fully-featured Star Wars space combat sim that, thanks to being a total conversion mod for Freespace Open, even runs on my rubbish laptop

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Star Wars has been back in the news recently because, to cut a long story short, the US alt-right have decided that Darth Vader was actually the good guy. We are not here to indulge such buffoonery. We are here to talk about Fate of the Galaxy, a free unofficial space combat simulator set during the events of the original Star Wars trilogy, featuring several mission-based campaigns and a stable of Imperial and Rebel Alliance ships that ranges from the quad-blasting X-Wing to Boba Fett's Firespray 31. Also, Naboo starfighters, if you really must.

It's a total conversion of poor dead Volition's venerable dogfighter FreeSpace 2, running on the open source Free Space 2 Open engine via the popular Knossos launcher. You don't need a copy of FreeSpace 2 to play it. You also don't need to pay. And nor do you need a whizbang PC – this bucket of bolts hums along happily on my i5-1145G7 with no dedicated graphics card. As the Rancor said to the padawan: finally, some good food.

Fate of the Galaxy has been in development off and on for almost two decades, weathering a number of team changes and shifts of focus. The FreeSpace Wiki has a longer account of its travels. It's not an officially licensed Star Wars production, as made evident by a scrolling text intro which takes the official orchestral theme and moves every note up or down a place, as though spitefully jolting the piano around under John Williams' fingers. It's not quite as bizarre as Portsmouth Sinfonia's Also sprach Zarathustra but it did make me cackle like the Emperor contemplating a freshly made asteroid field. But then I began playing, and my presumptuous merriment became a frown of concentration.

While hardly Steel Battalion, Fate of the Galaxy is a proper nerdy sim with a bustling 3D cockpit and all sorts of keyboard shortcuts to try once and immediately forget. You'll need to funnel energy between fore and aft shields during bombing runs on Star Destroyers, target individual subsystems with your ion cannon, give orders to other pilots, and consider whether to synch different guns for maximum effect. I can run the game fine on my work laptop with integrated graphics, but I'm definitely feeling the need for a beefier keyboard. And also a joystick. Certainly, I'm not going to get far in LAN multiplayer with this hunk of junk.

It seems a very generous production, based on 30 minutes playing kiss-chase with TIE bombers. The story campaigns and quickfix stand-alone skirmishes aside, there's a mission editor with text-based files for easier sharing. There's also VR support with head-tracking, should you own one of those haunted helmets. Learn more via the online manual (Google Doc), and find Fate of the Galaxy itself on Moddb. You'll need around seven gigs of disc space.

This being the year 2026, there's the question of generative AI usage - a topic of much concern lately in modding circles. Fate of the Galaxy's official site boasts that all the visual assets were "made by humans only". I'm less sure about other aspects – there's seemingly some artificial VO during the tutorial, though it sounds like antiquated, less insidious robocaller software. The FreeSpace Open engine itself greatly pre-dates today's chatbot invasion. The original FreeSpace 2 launched in 1999 - a more civilised age, when everybody still thought Darth Vader was an arsehole.

Researchers asked people to use AI chatbots for election info – results were mixed

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Chatbots can skew election-related information and act as an intervening layer in a voter's search.

Most search tools now put a layer of AI between you and the information you’re looking for, especially Google.

Also: Tired of AI Overviews? I found 9 Google Search alternatives

In a new study, Yale researchers found that a third of users asked a chatbot to help them pick a candidate. Grok often recommended candidates on its own without any information from users, while ChatGPT recommended candidates most often overall, and twice as much as Claude.

The researchers asked nearly 2,500 people in 35 states with Senate races to query ChatGPT, Claude, Grok, or Gemini about the election. Participants in the study, which ran for a few weeks in October leading up to the Nov. 3 midterm elections, were adults who already use chatbots weekly.

Most users asked chatbots for basic facts about the election, including who was on the ballot. But 36% asked a chatbot to help them pick a candidate to vote for, often by describing their priorities and asking the chatbot to assess which candidates aligned with them.

The former query has slightly less room for error, because the internet has a ton of data on the basic facts of an election, such as who is running where. Chatbots interpreting candidate positions, on the other hand, is a bit more dicey, and relies significantly on the sources a chatbot uses (a statement from a candidate’s own website, for example, is more reliable than a social media clip of an opponent characterizing them in a certain way).

Also: How to keep your chatbot conversations as private as possible

In one example, a user asked ChatGPT to give them the “pros and cons” of each candidate in Virginia, an inherently subjective question.

When it came to recommending candidates, chatbots varied widely.

“When users asked who to vote for but had not yet said anything about themselves, only Grok regularly recommended a candidate, in 14% of chats,” the researchers wrote. “Once users shared their views, ChatGPT recommended a candidate in 79% of chats and Claude in 40%, with Grok and Gemini in between.”

Pew Research found that 42% of US adults surveyed use chatbots to search for information. That’s a broad umbrella that can include election information — but Pew also found that only 8% of US adults reported using chatbots to find election-related information in this year’s election.

What those users are looking for matters, though. Pew found that of that group, 76% were inquiring about where candidates stand on certain issues, and 71% were fact-checking information they’d heard about candidates or issues.

Also: OpenAI will merge ChatGPT’s chat and work tabs

Given that chatbots are still not reliable summarizers, the nature of these questions poses a misinformation risk if users don’t also check a chatbot’s linked sources. An overwhelming majority of users don’t, at least when it comes to Google’s AI Overviews. At least one study found AI platforms gave users “outdated or inaccurate” information that could mislead voters in some cases by missing a deadline or failing to mention a step about voting logistics.

This election cycle was particularly plagued by AI-generated deepfakes and misinformation as tools have become much more sophisticated since the 2024 election. OpenAI and Anthropic reportedly set up election “war rooms” to monitor misuse and information accuracy, according to exclusive reporting by Politico and Semafor.

While an August study from the Brennan Center for Justice found that chatbots did push back surprisingly well against certain falsehoods related to election disinformation campaigns, it also found that AI models easily generated “misleading election-related images and video” when asked.

“Many of their safeguards for preventing this were ineffective, inconsistently applied, or functionally absent,” noted the study, which tested ChatGPT, Gemini, Grok, Meta AI, Runway, and Flux.2.

Also: 50 AI agents tried to build a thriving Minecraft civilization – it didn’t end well

Apart from these factors, however, there is a broader theme in AI use that is equally consequential for civic engagement: cognitive offloading. The political media landscape can already be dense and hard to parse for a voter trying to stay informed. Layering a chatbot — which has opaque training data sets, filters, and other settings determined at the discretion of AI labs — on top of that information search process can further muddy the waters while appearing like a convenient and neutral summarizing tool.

While some studies test how AI chatbots respond to election questions by using standardized questions, this approach let users themselves engage with chatbots as they normally would. While the Yale researchers’ queries and standardized study queries show a lot of similarities, the distinction is valuable for evaluations.

“This is a small step toward evaluations that measure AI’s political influence on the questions users actually ask,” the researchers noted.

Outside of users self-reporting what they asked a chatbot, only AI labs have records of what users are really asking about elections. In October, Anthropic analyzed 250,000 conversations with Claude and determined people “rarely ask Claude whom or what to vote for,” finding that “0.51% of all conversations are specifically about elections or voting,” though it expected election-related queries to increase closer to Nov. 3.

Also: 6 AI agent workflows I use to hand off everyday tasks

“More often, we observed people asking Claude questions that could help them reach their own conclusions, like where candidates stand on a particular issue. Requests that violated our election integrity policies were even less common, appearing in about 0.002% of conversations,” Anthropic’s report said.

The Yale researchers said the next step for a study like theirs is determining how accurate and unbiased models are in their responses, as well as what models learn about users, and whether their recommendations actually match users’ needs.

“In multi-turn chats, users often answered the chatbot’s questions, and those answers led to its recommendations. The questions a model asks shape what users tell it and what it recommends,” the researchers explained.

3 Expert-Approved Exercises to Build Power in Your Climbing

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

There’s a famous quote by Wolfgang Güllich, the German climber who invented the word “redpoint”: “There’s no such thing as too much power.” As a coach, I often see and hear this in the climbing community. I agree with the sentiment, but I think that by and large, power has been misused, misunderstood, and poorly trained by the vast majority of climbers.

Firstly, I think it’s misused and misunderstood because the general climbing population doesn’t grasp that strength and power are not the same thing. Secondly, as a result of this, the training methods climbers use when they’re trying to improve their power are often wrong.

So let’s start with a very simple explanation of strength and power. I’m also going to have to bring speed into the equation, as this gives context for how these all sit on a spectrum. Afterwards, we’ll look at some of the main things I think climbers are getting wrong when doing their own power training, how to solve these issues, and some examples of simple but effective ways to train power.

The reason that power matters so much in climbing is because not all moves are possible—or most efficient—when executed at a slower pace without speed and power. Trying to just use strength (slower movement) could mean that you’ll either fail on the move or you’ll climb inefficiently using excess energy, which ultimately means no send!

An easy example of this is completing a super long hard move. With just strength, we’re only able to lock to a certain depth of shoulder position and throw or deadpoint to a limited distance. Add in power and you’ll cover way more distance and end up latching that hold.

One additional note on the “power endurance” term: This one is a bit of a misnomer which caused us many issues over the years at Lattice, mainly because power endurance does not actually use power. It should have been named “strength endurance,” as that’s what it really is.

Strength is how much force you can produce. Think of a max hang on a small edge, or a heavy weighted pull-up. The movement is slow, but the force is high.

Speed is how fast you can move when there’s very little resistance. Think of how quickly you can move your hand from one hold to the next with only the weight of your own arm to overcome.

Power sits between the two. It’s force multiplied by speed, so you only have lots of it when you can produce a relatively high force quickly. Dynos, band-assisted pull-ups, and latching holds at the end of a deadpoint all fit into this category.

Think of it as a sliding scale. Heavy and slow climbing is at one end, light and fast climbing is at the other, and power is in the middle. You can’t produce your maximum force and your maximum speed at the same time, so where you train on that scale matters and I think climbers are continually getting this wrong.

None of this means strength doesn’t matter. Strength is the foundation of power. A stronger and bigger muscle can produce more force at every speed, so if you’re relatively weak, getting stronger will improve your power more than anything else. This is a really important point to grasp, for those of you who haven’t yet developed a foundation of high strength. Once you’re reasonably strong, though, specific power training starts to give you more benefits. As a rough guide, if you can’t do a pull-up with an extra 35-50% of your bodyweight, strength should be the main focus still (even though power training in addition is still useful). Once you’re above this threshold, power training should become increasingly important.

This is the big one. Power is extremely sensitive to fatigue and it drops well before you actually feel tired. Despite this, many climbers I see at the gym do their “power work” at the end of a session, after an hour of board climbing or a set of endurance drills. Unfortunately, if every rep is slower than your best, you’re practicing being slow. Power work goes at the start of the session, straight after a thorough warm-up, when you’re fresh. Prioritise this timing over everything.

Linked laps on the campus board, long sets of explosive pull-ups, and circuits of “powerful” moves can be useful, but they’re all mostly power endurance, not power. Power training means low numbers of high-quality reps. Stop the set as soon as a rep is noticeably slower, shorter, or messier than the first.

Ninety seconds between sets isn’t enough. You need at least three minutes, sometimes more, for the systems that drive maximal efforts to recover. If it feels like you’re resting way more than you should and you’re slightly bored, you’re probably resting the right amount.

The intention to move as fast as possible is a large part of what drives the adaptation, even when the movement itself ends up slow. So remember, if you’re doing a power pull-up, even if only the first 20% of the pull is fast, that’s ok. You should put in full intent, with complete focus on speed for each pull, and you’ll find that’s what really counts.

Some climbers only hang, push, squeeze, and lift heavy. Others love to campus, jump, spin, and dyno. The best results come from working across the scale. Heavy strength work (for the foundation of power and speed) and explosive power work should be included in the same training block, with the emphasis shifting depending on what you need for your goals.

Below are three power exercises that work well. Pick one or two for a session, do them first, and remember to stop as soon as speed or quality reduces.

If your 1RM pull-up is greater than 160%, you may be able to do this at bodyweight.

Pull as fast as possible, aiming to get your chest as high as possible, almost as if you’re trying to roll into a muscle up. Do four sets of three, resting three minutes between sets. The faster you can complete each rep, the better, so generally use more assistance than you might think.

Always learn how to do this session on a comfortable hold and using your preferred grip type.

Using a hangboard edge (or a load cell, if you have one), pull as hard and as fast as you can for two to three seconds. Do four to eight reps, resting at least three minutes in between. This trains your ability to generate high force quickly, which is exactly what deadpoints need.

These can be done on holds, a hangboard, or a pull-up bar. The critical part is getting the height of the starting position right.

Using your chosen hold, you want to jump from the ground into the middle range of the pull-up motion, using the momentum from your lower body to assist in generating speed and power in the upper part of the pull. You’re jumping into the pull-up with your legs, and your arms and back are finishing off the pull at high speed as you’re already carrying momentum. Do four to eight reps, resting at least three minutes in between.

Train power like this two to three times a week when it’s your focus, and once or twice a week to maintain it the rest of the time. Don’t forget that power fades faster than strength when you stop training it, so it’s worth keeping a small amount in your plan all year round.

So back to Güllich: Is there such a thing as too much power? Probably not. But there’s definitely such a thing as too much fatigue, too much volume, and too little speed and intention when you’re trying to train it. Get strong first, train power fresh in mind and body, keep the quality high, and stop before it drops. I think you’ll be pretty happy with how you feel on the wall after just a couple of months of this.

Unrest grows at Rotherham United after Tuesday night developments

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Rotherham United exited the Jersey Mike's Trophy in rather disappointing fashion on Tuesday night, losing to Newcastle United's Under-21s at the AESSEAL New York Stadium.

Alex Bruce's side needed a pick-me-up in the midst of an eight-game winless run in all competitions, which has seen them slide down towards the relegation zone in League Two, and a chance to progress in the EFL Trophy would have helped provide that.

Instead, a rotated, yet still filled with experienced senior names such as Dominic Iorfa, Brandon Williams, Kian Spence and Kladi Lolos, squad put in a meek showing against an academy side, who ran away 1-0 victors and secured second place in Northern Group C.

Pick any two players. Send the code, and your mate enters it here to play the same pair.

It's not been the best of seasons so far for Rotherham back in League Two, but this particular result has soured opinion on Bruce, COO Paul Douglas and chairman Tony Stewart even further.

Rotherham weren't exactly banging on the door of Newcastle's Under-21s on Tuesday night, despite being behind for close to an hour. The Millers had just nine shots, despite their 65% possession, and the Magpies academy actually had more on target over the course of the game.

Rotherham fans have, understandably, voiced their displeasure at the performance and the result online, with the majority of the ire directed at the club's hierarchy.

One supporter posted a photo with the caption, "Stewart and Douglas, killing our club," while another tweeted, "Against a team of kids and we looked softer than them. The clubs rotten. Stewart out #rufc"

"Can Tony Stewart please exit the club now. Ruined his legacy, purely for his own ego. Stewart out," was also amongst the comments, as was "Only the trophy but wow, that might be a new low... That's saying something with the last few years."

Arguably more damaging, though, was the tweet stating that the South Yorkshire side were "dead and buried," and "the worst side in England."

It's not uncommon for sides to fall from League One to League Two and struggle in the division below. More often than not, those clubs are in for another relegation battle than they are a promotion one, and in Carlisle United and Forest Green Rovers' case, they fall through the trapdoor once again and into non-league.

Rotherham look dangerously close to being one of the next sides to do that, but you get the feeling that the writing has been on the wall for quite some time now.

The Millers have been one of the more unstable clubs in the EFL for a while, bouncing up and down from League Two to the Championship and then yo-yoing from the second and third tiers over the last decade.

Do you think Rotherham United could get relegated again this season? Let us know in the comments.

Since their double promotion to the second tier in 2014, Rotherham haven't spent more than three years in one division. Many felt that a lengthy period in League One could provide some stability, but it would only be two years before they fell back into the fourth tier.

Now, the scary possibility of falling out of the Football League is on the horizon, and a lot of Millers fans are numb to the rapid regression of the club in recent seasons.

It's been a real mess at Rotherham over the past 12 months, but it's been highlighted more so in the last six.

From Lee Clark calling out the players' commitment in the latter stages of the League One campaign last season, to the intriguing implementation of a Head of Football and then hiring Steve McClaren to take that role, to the long, drawn-out process of hiring a new head coach which landed at Alex Bruce's feet, it's not been smooth sailing.

Bruce came into the club just days before pre-season started, with just 14 senior players confirmed to be staying on. He's had to navigate wholesale changes in the squad and nurse a relegation hangover in what is his first EFL managerial gig.

And ultimately, the underlying numbers, especially going forward, haven't read awfully. But the new boss can't account for the effort his players put in on the pitch and some of the mindless decisions that his players have made, too.

Bruce may pay for the loss against Newcastle's kids with his job, but, ultimately, it would take a real miracle-maker to turn Rotherham's fortunes around and get the fanbase back onside.

Barry Bannan reacts as Ali Al-Hamadi drops Sheffield Wednesday message

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Sheffield Wednesday striker Ali Al-Hamadi has taken to social media to reflect on his start to life at Hillsborough, and team-mate Barry Bannan has reacted to his post.

Al-Hamadi was one of 18 new signings to join Wednesday this summer as part of the club's rebuild under new owner David Storch, but it is fair to say his loan move from Ipswich Town has not gone as well as he would have hoped so far.

It has been an inconsistent start to life back in League One for Wednesday, with the South Yorkshire outfit currently sitting ninth in the table, two points from the play-off places, after winning just two of their last nine league games.

After parting company with Henrik Pedersen last month, the Owls appointed former Stockport County boss Dave Challinor as their new manager this week, and the 51-year-old is set to take charge for the first time in Sunday's FA Cup tie at Oldham Athletic.

However, while Challinor will be keen to run the rule over his new squad over the coming weeks, he may be without Al-Hamadi for the opening games of his tenure after he suffered a fresh injury blow.

Al-Hamadi is yet to open his account for Wednesday since his summer arrival, and injuries have limited him to just eight appearances so far, with his only start coming in the 2-0 defeat to Doncaster last month which proved to be Pedersen's final game.

After missing a few games earlier in the season with a foot problem, Al-Hamadi was not in the squad for Sunday's 1-1 draw with Blackpool due to a new injury, which manager Matt Hamshaw revealed could keep him out for "a number of weeks".

Unfortunately for Al-Hamadi, he has had his fair share of injury woes in recent years, particularly during his loan spell at Luton Town last season, where he scored just one goal and provided one assist in 14 appearances.

Al-Hamadi would have been to get a consistent run of games under his belt at Hillsborough and establish himself in the Wednesday team, but despite finding himself in the treatment room once again, it seems the Iraq international is determined to bounce back from his latest setback.

"Frustratingly, it has been stop-start the last few weeks for me personally," Al-Hamadi wrote on Instagram on Tuesday.

"Working as hard as ever to stay on that pitch, help the team and reach our goals for this season."

Al-Hamadi was inundated with support after his candid reflection on his time at Wednesday, with the post attracting over 16,600 likes at the time of writing, and among those was his Owls team-mate Barry Bannan.

Al-Hamadi has proven himself to be a prolific scorer in the EFL previously during his spell in League Two with AFC Wimbledon, but he has struggled to produce his best form since departing Plough Lane for Ipswich for a fee of just over £1 million almost three years ago.

Despite his injury problems at Luton last season, it is easy to see why Wednesday took a gamble on Al-Hamadi this summer, but as he continues to be plagued by fitness issues, his loan stint at Hillsborough is in danger of turning into another flop.

Should Ali Al-Hamadi be a regular starter for Sheffield Wednesday under Dave Challinor once he returns from injury? Tell us in the comments below!

With Challinor taking over this week, Al-Hamadi would no doubt have been keen to show the new Owls boss what he is capable of, but it appears he will be unavailable for the early stages of his reign, which could allow others to move ahead of him in the pecking order.
