The Best Laser Rangefinders for Hunting and Shooting, Tested and Reviewed

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 4, 2026 10:29 AM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

Most of us judge a laser rangefinder by its obvious attributes. When we peer through the lens and press the ranging button, does it deliver an accurate and consistent distance to the target. Done and done. But when it comes to sorting out the best rangefinders for hunting, we need to dig a bit deeper.

A few curious members of our community navigate the mode commands, and learn that their range-u-lator can also measure incline, temperature, elevation, and altitude and apply a prodigious amount of ballistic calculation to make us more lethal hunters.

Ours is a test of these multidimensional monoculars, those that carry ballistic software, ranging reticles, and even navigation capability. Because not every hunter and shooter needs or wants that capability, we include a few point-and-shoot units fully worth your attention. This year’s field was among the most exciting we’ve seen with both high value and the latest technology. Here are our picks for best rangefinders for hunting from our 2026 optics test.

Garmin’s entry into the laser rangefinding market has had such a seismic effect on the category that once you use the L60i, LRF monoculars that simply measure distance and spit out a ballistic solution are so two-dimensional. By adding an augmented reality display that leverages Garmin’s extensive digital mapping services, the rangefinder becomes a first-rate wayfinder.

It’s impossible to overstate the ambition of this device. Garmin has managed to pack so much mapping horsepower in this handheld unit that it is being used by search-and-rescue agencies to locate wayward folks, by explorers who want to see what’s on the other side of a blind ridge, and by hunters who want step-by-step navigation to the spot they sighted that cross-canyon buck, even if they approach from the opposite direction.

The L60i communicates its mapping capabilities via a see-through overlay inside the display that seems at times like it’s an alternative-reality headset. You can see the real world of terrain through the glass of the optical system, but the display drops over the seen landscape a digital veil of landmarks, distances, navigation arrows, and waypoints. Operationally, this means you can find waypoints from any direction. Practically, it means the Garmin’s distance isn’t limited by its laser beam but by its optical gaze. If you can see a landmark, even 20 miles away, you can drop a waypoint on it using Garmin’s onboard map.

But the unit is also a first-rate laser rangefinder. Garmin rates its ranging capabilities in two very different conditions: full sunlight and optimal conditions. Those of us who have used laser rangefinders in full daylight know that the laser bounces off all sorts of reflective objects, limiting its precision. To get the best distance readings, you want to laze objects under cloudy skies or just after the sun has set. Garmin claims the L60i can range reflective targets out to 7,500 yards in optimum conditions, or to 5,500 yards in full sunlight. We got nearly those readings in our testing.

The Garmin also has a first-rate ballistics solver onboard, thanks to its long relationship with and now ownership of Applied Ballistics. It leverages the integrated AB Elite software or AB Quantum software via the Garmin app to provide not only ballistic groupings but the specific performance of custom loads out of your particular rifle. It links to Kestrel wind meters. It includes an archery mode and an extended-range ballistics mode for ranging out to nearly 8,000 yards. But it also links to the universe of Garmin devices, from dozens of smart watches to the company’s myriad GPS devices.

The unit is among the largest and heaviest (13.6 ounces with twin AAA batteries on board) LRF monoculars we’ve tested, but that heft settles nicely in hand. To get the best out of the unit’s extended range, mount it to a tripod via the threaded adapter on the underside of the monocular. But hand-holding the unit is the best way to enjoy its mapping features, because the heads-up overlay moves seamlessly with the landscape as you pan. The Garmin is at its best with both the rangefinding and mapping features employed. Laze a distant target, acquire the ballistic solution to make a shot (or not), then drop a waypoint on the target location. Now, employ the unit’s graphic overlay to not only “see” the waypoint on a topo map, but actually navigate to it, even if you approach from a different direction. Highlighted prompts give you step-by-step directions to the waypoint. The effect is surreal, as though you are watching yourself from the perspective of an eagle. Or a satellite.

This out-of-body experience is not only useful for the pedestrian tasks of hitting targets and retrieving game, but also for exploring your world both in your boots and from far above. By harnessing the best attributes of Garmin’s mapping library, applying an excellent ballistics package to the task, and then connecting both through devices, apps, and digital ecosystems, Garmin has elevated the LRF monocular to something that approaches the first do-everything handheld device on the market.

Outdoor Life’s testers approached the Garmin L60i with a dose of skepticism. Could an LRF monocular really drop waypoints 20 miles or more away? Could its heads-up display really be visible enough to be useful? And wasn’t that $2,500 price tag simply too precious? By the end of our test, every question had been resolved to our satisfaction, excepting the cliff-hanger: Whatever in the world will Garmin dream up next?

For its astonishing capabilities, its ability to perform as a ballistics-solving LRF at a high level, and the audacious ambition of the device, the Garmin L60i is the consensus winner of Editor’s Choice as the standout LRF monocular of the year.

An 850-yard rangefinder for under $100? That’s not only about 12 cents per yard, it’s a crazy value for a starter monocular or for hunters and shooters who don’t often shoot over a quarter mile.

The Triumph HD 850 continues Vortex’s product line that provides good performance and amazing value, with full warranty coverage. The Triumph line announced itself with the very good 10×42 binocular that has a real-world street price of about $100. Like the binocular, the HD 850 will get the job done, assuming the job is mid-distance ranging, without a lot of extraneous features or particularly good optics.

Vortex claims a range of 850 yards on reflective targets and 350 yards on game. We actually got pretty close, ranging metallic targets out to 680 yards and deer at 325 yards. The LRF has the typical modes: normal, first, and last target modes and line-of-sight and angle-adjusted ranging mode, along with a fairly sluggish scan mode.

The unit has a liquid crystal display that appears washed out against some backgrounds. The ranging engine is noticeably slower than the top LRFs in our test, but it’s just fine for most purposes. Note that the Triumph HD 850 doesn’t have a ballistic solver or location pinning or any of the features you might see in a unit that links to a phone app. But its angle-compensating mode and minimum ranging distance of 6 yards makes it a good choice for bowhunters. It’s also a good unit for shotgun hunters, straight-wall carbiners, and other hunters and shooters who shoot large-caliber bullets that drop quickly, and where knowing the specific yardage inside 300 yards is the difference between a clean miss and a killing shot.

The optics are pretty tough, and the plastic unit feels a little flimsy, but its 4.6-ounce weight and compact build make it a good choice for go-light hunters or a treestand hunter who doesn’t want, or need, a heavy ballistic-calculating rangefinder.

For all its remarkable value packed in a no-nonsense build and its ability to perform the basics we require of a laser rangefinding monocular, the Vortex Triumph HD 850 wins our Great Buy award as the great bargain of the year in laser rangefinders.

Bushnell has added some tasty updates to this workhorse LRF. The Broadhead 2 has Bushnell’s very good proprietary weatherproofing coatings that boost contrast and should make the unit more usable in drippy weather. Speaking of grubby conditions, the LRF’s Brush/Fog mode, which prioritizes farther targets, has a powerful update in this generation of the Broadhead. It’s a logic that discourages the laser from picking up reflections off water droplets, and Bushnell’s works quite well.

The second-generation Broadhead retains Bushnell’s ActiveSync display that sounds on the surface like a gimmick but is a very useful feature. Sensitive to light intensity, it runs black LCD in daylight conditions but automatically turns to a red illumination in twilight. It’s a clutch attribute that allows the display to pop against any background or light condition.

We really like the overbuilt VaultLite rangefinder pouch, which has useful hardware for belts, and we like the pistol grip of the unit. The protruding outline of the rearward battery compartment serves as a “hook” that grabs the hand, whether gloved or not, and helps stabilize the very light (6-ounce) monocular.

And we like the tripod mount on the belly of the unit. That’s a nice touch for those using the Broadhead at its most-distant ranges when precision is important.

The Broadhead 2 didn’t especially wow us with its optics, ranging horsepower, or menu navigation. All are fine, and just what you should expect from laser rangefinders at this price and ranging engine.

It may take users a bit of time to get used to Bushnell’s nomenclature. “Regular” mode is line-of-sight mode. “Bow” mode is the angle-compensating mode, which Bushnell calls its “ARC” feature. “Fog” mode is called “last target” by some manufacturers and Bushnell’s “Bullseye” mode is what most LRF users know as “first target” mode.

The Bushnell R5 Broadhead 2 is a great choice for bowhunters and rifle hunters and shooters who need to reach out to uncomfortable distances. The lack of a ballistic solver holds it back from being used by shooters who require more of an LRF than just range. But it’s a solid rangefinder in the middle of a very competitive pack.

The Gen II version of Sig’s excellent Kilo8K-ABS rangefinding monocular is more powerful, brighter, and connects to a larger galaxy of mapping and ballistics apps. In other words, it’s almost a brand-new laser rangefinder.

Its engine even got an upgrade. The Kilo8K-ABS Gen II runs on Sig’s new LightWave DSP, which returns four readings a second in scan mode, even out to a mile. Its return is snappy, precise, and confident, and new coatings and optics make the active-matrix display pop. The display is worth a few sentences. Some manufacturers are so busy crowding information in their display that the result is a confusing thicket of distance, ballistics solutions, angle, temperature, and mode. Sig has done a nice job of simplifying the display to just the elements needed to make a quick shot: the range, and the come-up and wind holds. In the field, it’s fast and simple.

The lazing power of the unit is simply stunning. In extended-range mode and strapped to a tripod, I was able to range grassy hillsides at 6,200 yards without much trouble. I worked on getting the claimed 8,000 yards out of the unit, but couldn’t quite get there. The impressive part to me isn’t the distance, it’s the speed of the DSP engine.

The onboard Applied Ballistics Elite software is similarly impressive. You can tack up the data by connecting to the AB Quantum app, but you don’t need to. The entire ballistics library is in the palm of your hand, almost quivering in the 7.5-ounce monocular that’s begging to be put to work. That the unit also connects to Sig’s BDX devices, including BDX-enabled riflescopes, gives this monocular even more utility.

There are a lot of choices on the market for ballistics-solving LRF monoculars. At first glance, the price of the 8K-ABS Gen II (just under $1,500) seems excessive, but when you consider its ability to quickly and precisely range, then deliver an equally fast and verified aiming solution out to 4.5 miles … I don’t know that I’d call the Sig a bargain, but it’s a very good deal for hunters and shooters who intend to take it to its impressive limits.

We’ve seen Leupold’s RX-5000 before. Two years ago, we gushed about its ability to remotely drop onX pins just by lazing a distant spot and then pinning the location to the digital map on your phone. It’s a useful feature, but Leupold’s competitors have made it standard on so many LRF monoculars and binoculars that it’s no longer novel.

But a lot has changed with this little handheld marvel since then. Its ballistics solvers are now powered by Hornady’s excellent 4DOF software, its bow mode borrows from Precision Cut Archery software, and its mapping feature is not limited to onX Hunt. You can drop pins on maps from Deercast, HuntStand, and both Apple and Google maps. Those updates are enough to include it in this roundup of new LRF monoculars because, while the form factor and operation are pretty much the same, the guts of the RX-5000 are new and galaxies more powerful and useful than the original.

That form factor is worth mentioning. While many sub-$400 LRF monoculars feel flimsy, the Leupold has a tight, metallic heft that settles nicely in hand. We wish the unit had a tripod adapter built in, but the tripod “saddle” that Leupold ships is a good addition, and it’s necessary to get the most out of the RX-5000’s long-range chops. With the unit stabilized on a tripod, we were able to get ranges very nearly to 5,000 yards, and when you add Hornady’s 4DOF ballistic solver to that extended distance, it’s both imaginable and possible to hit targets at that extreme distance.

The optics in the Leupold are excellent. The 8-power magnification combined with the relatively large 30mm objective make this a pretty good glassing unit. But the value proposition, of course, is the ballistics paired with the fast and precise ranging engine. Given the huge amount of ballistic computations, the powerful laser, the excellent Leupold Control app, and generally clear and intuitive navigation, you could make the case that the 799RX-5000issotalentedthatitcouldundercuttheequallyimpressivebutpricey(3,900) BX-6 Range HD, Leupold’s new LRF binocular.

With this do-everything long-range LRF monocular, Leica has regained its standing at the head of the rangefinding line. The Rangemaster CRF Max not only compares favorably to peers from Sig and Leupold, but its build and optics borrow from its extensive history in the category.

But it’s the electronic guts of the Rangemaster CRF Max, introduced in 2025 but hitting retailers’ shelves now, that elevate the device. Leica has stuffed the monocular with a best-in-class ballistics solver (Applied Ballistics Elite), an interesting should-I-or-shouldn’t-I shot-probability calculator that gives you the odds of making extra-long shots, a location feature to drop waypoints on digital maps, and a tracking tool that guides shooters from the place they took the shot to the location of the target.

For just under $1,500, that’s a lot of performance packed in a tight, elegant build. The flat dark earth colorway is a giveaway to its talents. Leica has started to use device color to define its place in the brand pantheon. The base Rangemaster CRF is onyx black, the mid-level Rangemaster CRF Pro is forest green, and the top-of-the-line Rangemaster CRF Max has this handsome FDE exterior.

The unit has all the ranging features you’d expect, including three target modes, the ability to input wind direction and intensity or connect to an external anemometer, and the ability to use either onboard ballistics or import custom ballistic data from Leica’s excellent mobile app.

The 3,700-yard maximum range for the device may not compete with Sig’s 8,000-yard model or Leupold’s 5,000-yard monocular, but it’s plenty powerful for nearly any long-range hunting or shooting situation. We managed to get very nearly that distance when ranging hillsides and reflective signs.

The laser return is fast, the display is clear and bright and customizable, so you can include only the data you need to make quick, confident shots. And the glass and coatings are superior, as you’d expect from an optics company with the performance expectations of Leica.

If Bushnell’s Broadhead 2 is purpose-built for bowhunters, the 2000 AB version is similarly configured for rifle hunters. To have baked-in Applied Ballistics’ solver in a $250 rangefinder is pretty extraordinary.

The value proposition of this new R5 rangefinder is pretty appealing. Once you build a ballistic profile for your rifle load in the Bushnell Ballistics mobile app, then connect via Bluetooth, the rangefinder will give you a distance-informed hold in MOA or MIL. Note that the Ultralite version of Applied Ballistics is valid only out to 800 yards, but that’s plenty enough horsepower for most hunters and long-range shooters.

The pros and cons that we discussed in the Broadhead 2 all apply to the 2000 AB, with the exception of the display. While the archery R5 contains Bushnell’s excellent ActiveSync display, the rifle version has only a black LCD. We also note that the 2000 AB is powered by a single CR2 lithium battery. We’d like to see a CR123-sized powerplant for extended use given the amount of data the little unit has to crunch.

The R5 2000 AB retails for $250, but you can expect to see that price come down as the unit wins more fans and as more customers learn about the crazy amount of performance in such a simple and affordable rangefinding monocular.

Manufacturers’ ranging claims are as spurious as a camouflage company’s promise to elude. Most take their best distances, not their average, and discount target size and orientation. They don’t always report what we found, some inconsistent ranging on a wide variety of surfaces, from gravel to grass to 3D archery targets to trees. We test the maximum and minimum range, and use every targeting mode and menu option. We’ve provided the max and minimum ranges we were able to achieve on non-reflective targets to give you an idea of real-world capabilities.

We mount monoculars on tripods to get rock-solid ranging, and we take the units in the field, deploying them both methodically and rapidly at targets of various sizes and distances. And then we pair them to a slave rifle and a ballistic app and judge their ability to improve our shooting accuracy.

We break our 10-point scoring into four general categories: ranging performance, display and optics, features, and value. The average of these categories is the basis of our grades, detailed below.

Ranging performance includes both the maximum and minimum distances the unit is capable of ranging, but also ranging precision and ability to repeatedly range objects of various sizes and textures. The performance score also considers various targeting modes. Display and optics assesses how well the unit “sees” targets, how useful and adjustable the display is to each users’ eyesight and different light conditions, and reticle utility. The feature grade assesses whether the unit connects to a mobile app, or contains a ballistic library, or has on-board sensors. Lastly, our value grade is a measure of whether the unit is worth its price, and considers its peer group, warranty, and amenities like a lanyard or carrying case.

The laser rangefinder that gets the highest overall score wins our Editor’s Choice award for the best in the category; the optic with the highest price/value score wins our Great Buy recognition. We also assigned other awards for specific noteworthy attributes.

Our 10-point evaluation adds up to a total numeric score, but we translate those to grades for each submission. The ranging Performance grade combines the scores from ranging, accuracy, sensitivity, and processor speed. Our Display/Optics grade aggregates both display and optics scores. The Features grade considers amenities, connectivity, and versatility. And then the Price/Value grade is our assessment of whether a rangefinder is worth the money, which also considers durability.

To earn an “Excellent” grade, the average of that category must be 9 or higher, which is extremely hard to achieve. “Very Good” is an average score of 7 to 9. A “Good” grade is 5 to 7. Our “Fair” grade is 3 to 5, and “Poor” is anything under 3. This scoring system gives us a great comparative tool for the best rangefinders for hunting.

As you can deduce from the wide variety of capabilities in submission to this year’s LRF test, you can spend over $1,000 for a gee-whiz rangefinder, or you can spend $200 for a basic unit that will range your target even if it doesn’t make you breakfast.

It’s up to you to determine what you want out of a rangefinding monocular. Do you want a hand-held ballistic calculator that will give you precise ranging out to 2 miles for your pet load? Or are you satisfied with a basic unit that provides line-of-sight and angle-adjusted ranging and little else?

Of course, there are plenty of units that reside between those two poles, but the biggest differentiator for LRFs in this year’s test are those that borrow from a mobile app to provide very specific customized aiming solutions, and those that don’t allow for many inputs and instead spit out ranges for a few basic hunting and shooting scenarios.

The price between those two capabilities is immense, with Bluetooth-connected units selling for close to $1,000 and bare-bones LRFs retailing for under $300.

Beyond those basics, if you are mainly a bowhunter who wants archery-specific ranging data, look for those units that have arrow-flight dynamics baked into their software. Similarly, suppose you’re an ultra-long-distance target shooter. In that case, you’re going to want a unit with precise, situation-informed ranging results based around the specific bullet dynamics you’ve measured and plugged into the ranging software.

Check out the display of a rangefinder you’re interested in in a wide variety of light conditions. Some units have blazingly bright OLED displays while others have black LCD displays that can get lost against a cluttered background or low-light situations.

Lastly, consider the warranty. Unlike the lifetime, fully transferable warranty that has become an industry standard for non-electronic optics like binoculars and rifle scopes, most companies offer a limited warranty for electronics like rangefinders. But there’s such a wide range of protection on the market that it pays to take a close look at the details.

There are a lot of great rangefinders for hunting. To find the best one for you, you need to consider how, where, and what you hunt.

Bushnell rangefinders are popular, especially because they are affordable. But there are also other options that are better in some ways.

Yes, a rangefinder is an incredibly valuable tool for hunting.

That depends. Cheap rangefinders can work great if you only need to range shorter distances under 200 yards or so, and you won’t be subjecting them to bad weather or too much moisture. For some hunters, they will get the job done.

Most of us use only a single laser rangefinder button—the ranging button—and as long as we get fast, reliable range dope, we’re happy. That’s actually how we started our assessment, simply by punching the range button and recording the results.

But the cool thing about the best rangefinders for hunting is that they are so adaptive. The most sophisticated have on-board or off-board ballistics solvers that can guide a bullet well beyond reasonable ranges. The best units have environmental sensors and sensitive angle and directional intelligence that can inform a shot whether you’re connected to your phone, or not. And the expectation of a LRF these days is that it must have both line-of-sight and angle-adjusted ranging along with ballistically informed ranging.

If you don’t need the full-on connected unit, make sure the unconnected unit performs the basics well, with an intensity adjusted display, an ergonomic body, and enough features to enable ranging a buck deer standing behind a screen of branches. The only downside to this crop of capable rangefinders is that they’re removing our last excuse for missing that must-hit shot.

Andrew McKean is Outdoor Life’s hunting and conservation editor, drilling into issues that affect wildlife, wildlands, and the people who care about them. He’s also OL’s optics editor, helping readers to make informed buying decisions.

After an Accident Left Her Paralyzed, Brittany Chadbourne Found a Way to Keep Climbing

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

“I'm the giant asterisk at the end of ‘climbing is dangerous.'" —Brittany Chadbourne (Photo: Maya Silver)

For some climbers, campusing—climbing with only your upper body—is a training strategy. For Utah climber Brittany Chadbourne, it’s her only choice.

I met Chadbourne in early October at Momentum Millcreek, one of the gyms she regularly visits in the Salt Lake Valley. She pulled onto a V3 boulder problem angled at least 45 degrees, working her way to the top and back down in about a minute. Then she eased herself into her wheelchair, hardly out of breath.

Bouldering carries particular risks for Chadbourne. Because she can’t control how she lands, a fall could cause additional injuries, including damage to the rods in her spine. These days, she saves harder climbing for ropes and sticks to boulder problems she knows she can safely descend. Afterward, I belayed the 28-year-old on a 5.11c. Her powerful shoulders, arms, and back carried her up the route without the use of her legs or lower abs.

Four years ago, a routesetting accident left Chadbourne paralyzed from the belly button down, so she had to find a new way to practice the sport she loves. “I’ve always wanted to be in Climbing Magazine,” Chadbourne told me. “But I thought it would be for something way cooler than a V2 boulder. I’m still proud that I’m continuing with it and being positive, or at least as positive as I can be.”

Chadbourne discovered climbing while in school at Weber State University in Ogden, Utah, and really got into it in 2020. But before she started climbing, while growing up in northern Utah, she was a gymnast who had heard plenty of stories about gymnasts breaking their necks and becoming paralyzed.

“It was something that I always feared and tried to avoid,” Chadbourne says of paralysis.

On November 22, 2022, that fear became reality.

When Chadbourne began working as a routesetter in September of that year at The Front in Ogden, routesetters used a pull-through system of static lines, an alpine butterfly knot, and quickdraw anchors to access the wall. Chadbourne says the setup made her nervous from the start. She worried the knot could slip through the quickdraw carabiners. And the system differed from the one she’d used while routesetting at Weber State University.

“I was pretty new to all of it. So I was just like, ‘If this is what you guys have been using, obviously it’s safe. I’m sure it’s fine.’ Obviously, it wasn’t fine.”

Not long after she started, Chadbourne and The Front have stated that another routesetter reattached the wrong side of the rope when they were lowering and fell from the wall. According to both The Front and Chadbourne, the climber wasn’t seriously injured. After the accident, The Front retired the system and hosted trainings to get all routesetters up to speed on the new standards. Eddie Morillas, the gym’s director of routesetting, told Climbing via email that an updated fall-protection plan took effect on October 18, 2022, removing the alpine butterfly setup.

Under the new standard, Morillas explained that routesetters “are only permitted to gain initial access to anchors and remove working lines while on belay or connected to an autobelay unit, where applicable.” He added that Chadbourne attended two training sessions on the new standard before her accident.

According to Chadbourne, the head routesetter that day instructed her to use the alpine butterfly setup. The Front confirmed this, stating in an email to Climbing: “Our understanding is that the routesetter in charge let Brittany use the alpine butterfly system, which went against The Front’s fall protection plan and the training every setter on the team had completed.”

The precise cause of Chadbourne’s fall remains uncertain. Morillas told Climbing that The Front’s investigation found her knots were tied correctly, but that she pulled the rope in the wrong direction, preventing the knot from cinching around the anchor. According to The Front, the knot temporarily caught in the anchor before slipping through as Chadbourne moved up the rope.

Chadbourne says she followed the head routesetter’s instructions when setting up her static line correctly and had a coworker double-check it. Near the top of the wall, as she slid her ascender upward, she felt the rope go slack. She believes the knot slipped through the quickdraws, just as she’d feared.

“I immediately knew that I was falling,” she says. “So I just closed my eyes and fell 30 feet.”

When Chadbourne hit the ground, her left leg took most of the impact. She shattered her tibia and fibula, and suffered a burst fracture of her T12 vertebra and compression fractures at her C7, T5, and T7 vertebrae. The fall left her paralyzed from the belly button down.

Chadbourne says she receives workers’ compensation benefits that cover her medical expenses and equipment, including wheelchairs. She has chosen not to pursue a lawsuit, saying she was already overwhelmed by the aftermath of the accident and didn’t believe legal action would change what had happened.

Initially, Chadbourne felt embarrassed that her accident happened in a gym rather than outdoors. She’d spent years caving, exploring abandoned mines, and climbing outside without serious injury, only to suffer a life-altering fall inside a gym.

But that embarrassment didn’t diminish her desire to climb.

“I pretty much knew immediately that I would go back into climbing,” Chadbourne says. “Even as I was being loaded onto the helicopter to go to the hospital.” She didn’t know a lot about paraclimbing, but she figured there must be people out there climbing without their legs. “Campusing is a thing, so I’m sure somebody’s doing it,” she recalls thinking.

After two months in the hospital and another month adjusting to her new normal at home, Chadbourne’s doctor cleared her to climb. But her first time back on belay was “pretty rough.” She made it about 10 feet off the ground, partially hoisted up by her belayer. At first, she couldn’t climb high enough to experience the fear she’d felt during her fall. But as she grew stronger and began climbing higher, Chadbourne admits some of the fear came back.

Chadbourne still has days when she cries about her injury, sometimes even while climbing.

“Day to day, my life is just as hard now as it was four years ago [when I was first injured],” she adds. “I’m just doing more.” And she is. She’s traveling more, training more, and trying things she once thought were out of reach, including becoming an ambitious competitive climber.

Before the accident, Chadbourne loved technical slab climbing. Now, without the use of her legs or lower abdomen, she competes in the AL1 paraclimbing classification, also known as seated climbing.

In 2024, just two years after her injury, Chadbourne made Team USA. She earned bronze in the women’s AL1 competition at the 2025 Paraclimbing National Championships and silver in 2026.

But she found herself in a small competitive field. With so few women in the AL1 category, Chadbourne often faced just one or two other athletes. She and fellow paraclimber Carlie Cook advocated for mixed-gender AL1 competition, which would allow women and men to compete against each other. In March 2025, the International Federation of Sport Climbing released updated paraclimbing guidelines permitting mixed-gender competition in the category.

Competing against a larger field has made the sport more exciting for Chadbourne. “If it was just women, it would be like three or four of us in the entire world,” she says. “So to be able to compete against 20-plus people is really exciting. And it makes me want to beat the men!”

Now, Chadbourne has her sights set on making a World Cup final one day. She’s also working to bring more athletes with paraplegia, especially women, into the sport. One day, she hopes there will be enough female competitors to support a separate women’s category.

But there’s another stage Chadbourne hopes to compete on someday: the Paralympics.

“When I had heard rumors that [paraclimbing] was going to be in the LA Games, I got really excited,” Chadbourne recalls. But like many paraclimbing categories, AL1 was excluded from climbing’s Paralympic debut at the Los Angeles 2028 Games.

Chadbourne hopes that will change for the 2032 Paralympics in Australia. In the meantime, she’s also training in Nordic skiing and biathlon, with ambitions of making Team USA in both sports.

For Chadbourne, staying busy is one way of coping with the difficult days. “The busier you are, your brain just forgets to be sad,” she says.

“Continual motion builds on itself,” she adds. “I don’t really have any other choice but to just keep on going.”

Chadbourne’s momentum has carried into her work with other climbers. Between competitions and training, she teaches climbing and continues to set routes at Weber State University.

There, she’s become an advocate for safe routesetting and climbing practices, encouraging climbers to ask questions about systems before trusting them.

“I’m the giant asterisk at the end of ‘climbing is dangerous,’” Chadbourne says about teaching college students. “I want to prepare them that this is a possibility, but then I prepare them to avoid it in as many ways as they can.”

The most important lesson Chadbourne underscores is to always speak up if you don’t understand how something works. No matter their experience level, Chadbourne wants all climbers to feel comfortable raising concerns and asking questions.

“I want them to feel like they have a voice.”

Tactics Column: High alert at the back stick

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A long overdue win at Anfield was marked by a goal, a number of other Arsenal chances, and what will go down as a famous clean sheet, all involving how the two teams defend at the back post.

There were plenty of other interesting things to note on Sunday – Martin Odegaard’s role drifting across the pitch early on and Arsenal’s 20-odd minute spell of dominance midway through the second half both caught the eye – but in the latest instalment of our video series Between The Lines, I look into how Arsenal had great joy attacking the back post, and why Liverpool were never going to have the same luck at the other end.

With the hosts’ defence getting narrow in their own box, Arsenal created plenty of opportunities by having the man on the far side outside that defensive block. That’s how Kai Havertz scored, it’s how he nearly tapped in from Bruno Guimaraes’ effort that flew just wide, and it’s how Noni Madueke nearly teed Havertz up with another tap-in. It’s also where Christos Tzolis was positioned as he came onto a Madueke cross and forced Alisson into the game’s standout save.

Arsenal, though, have no such weakness: their combination of organisation, hunger, and (perhaps most importantly) focus meant Liverpool were never likely to find a spare man at the back post.

Think your vegetable garden is finished for the year? Not so fast. Some crops relish cooler temperatures and can shrug off a little frost.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

November doesn’t necessarily mark the end of the vegetable growing season. While warm-season crops are definitely done for the year, you may still be able to sow and grow some of your cold-hardy favorites.

Knowing how and when to plant a vegetable garden so late in the season depends on your USDA hardiness zone, climate, and local weather. If you have milder winters, you can likely sow seeds directly in outdoor beds. In colder climates, you’ll need to provide some protection against frost and snow.

Here, I offer pro tips on late veggie growing, along with eight vegetables you may be able to start now for a winter or spring harvest. If you’re unsure about whether or not you can grow any of these in November, contact your extension office for more location-specific guidance.

Planting veggies now takes more effort than simply sowing seeds. Beyond knowing your climate and hardiness zone, you need to pay attention to the weather. For example, avoid seeding when the soil is frozen or soggy.

Prepare the soil by clearing out weeds, raking out any stones or remaining summer plant debris, and mixing in fresh compost. Seed planting depth and spacing depend on the crop you are sowing, so do check the seed packet information.

Manage your expectations for this project. Less sunlight and colder temperatures mean germination and growth will be slow. Be patient.

Although these veggies are either cold-tolerant or good at overwintering, they still will not tolerate very cold weather. If you have cold winters, you’ll need to protect your efforts. An old milk jug can give seeds some protection and act as a mini greenhouse for a few seeds. If you want to start more seeds, use a cold frame, like the Giantex wooden cold frame, which has a flip top for easy access.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

As plants develop, row covers will protect winter veggies from frost, snow, harsh winds, and flying pests. This Plant Covers Freeze Protection Kit on Amazon includes hoops, clips, and a roll of frost blanket.

Apply straw, shredded leaves, or other loose, organic mulch over and around new crops or overwintering veggies. Just take care not to drown delicate seedlings in mulch.

Take care with watering, as letting soil get soggy is easy to do in winter. Check the soil before watering plants.

These are some of our favorite vegetables that many gardeners can start in November, including those that could be ready in winter and others that you’ll harvest in spring or summer.

Garlic is an example of a crop that will overwinter and provide you with a harvest next summer. As long as your soil is workable and not waterlogged, garlic is a dependable crop for planting now. Plant individual garlic cloves with the pointy ends up. Use mulch to protect the cloves from winter freezes. If successful, your cloves will grow roots over winter, foliage in spring, and bulbs in the summer.

You can plant garlic from the grocery store, though it’s usually a softneck variety grown in milder climates and may struggle in colder regions. Hardneck garlic is often a better choice for those areas. Botanical Interests offers an organic garlic flavor sampler bundle featuring two hardneck varieties and elephant garlic.

Shallots can also be overwintered and will reward you with a harvest in late spring or early summer. Plant shallot sets or individual shallot bulbs with the pointy ends up, just slightly above the soil surface. French Red Shallots from Burpee are a delicious culinary variety worth trying.

You should mulch lightly to protect shallots in colder climates. However, fall planting is best suited to milder regions, and if you have very cold winters, the planting window may already have passed. Check with your local extension office for exact dates.

Spinach is remarkably cold-hardy. The seeds will be slow to germinate in the cold, though. This Sow Right Seeds Spinach Seed Collection contains three tasty varieties. Unless you have very mild winters, use cold frames or other protections. Once spinach plants get established, they should produce leaves you can pick throughout the winter. Harvest from the outside edge of the plants, leaving the center intact.

Kale is another very cold-hardy vegetable that many gardeners can grow now for winter harvests. It’s also hard to grow from seed in the cold, so unless you have mild weather in November, consider using transplants, like these Bonnie Plants Lacinato Kale Plants from Lowe's. Use cold frames or row covers as needed, especially during hard freezes. For warmer zones, Burpee sells a four-variety kale blend.

Arugula is a mildly cold-tolerant green that is best for growing in November in milder climates. If conditions are right, it will germinate and grow rapidly, giving you a winter harvest. If you want to grow arugula in colder regions, use frames or a greenhouse and be prepared for growth to be slower. You can pick up a pack of arugula seeds for less than $5 from Sow Right Seeds.

Like arugula, radishes grow rapidly, so if the weather and soil conditions are right, you can start plants now and get a quick winter harvest. Burpee's Cooks Custom Blend Radish Seeds Collection contains red, pink, purple, and white radishes. For the best results, the soil should be workable, and November temperatures should be cool or mild. For colder areas, plant the most cold-tolerant varieties and use cold frames.

Plant carrots now for a sweet treat in early spring. Carrots generally overwinter well, but choose the more cold-tolerant varieties for the best results. Scarlet Nantes, included in this Gardeners Basics 5-Variety Pack on Amazon, can be harvested through winter with protection. It’s also important to make sure the soil is loose and free of stones, so the roots can grow deep. Use a generous layer of mulch to keep the soil warmer over winter.

Peas can also generally be overwintered for a spring harvest. Peas grow best in cooler weather, but they still need some warmth to establish. Alaska peas, available from Eden Brothers, tolerate chilly conditions, but if your November is very cold, it might be too late. In milder growing regions, this can be a good time to start peas. Be sure to plant your peas near a trellis or other vertical structure so the plants can climb in spring.

November doesn’t have to mean the end of growing veggies for the year. Depending on your region and local weather, you may be able to grow or overwinter some or all of these cold-tolerant vegetables. Choose the plants most suited to your conditions and be patient. Colder weather means slower growth.

Data centers are learning to be flexible

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Data centers are driving up electricity demand, but flexible power use could ease grid congestion and speed up connections

Jim West/UCG/Universal Images Group via Getty Images

A version of this article originally appeared in Quartz’s AI & Tech newsletter. Sign up here to get the latest AI & tech news, analysis and insights straight to your inbox.

Picture a soccer club that bought a passenger van for its tournaments a few times a year. The van sits in a driveway the rest of the year. Buying it was the right call, because a van that wasn’t there on tournament weekend would have stranded the team.

The American power grid works the same way, and now we have numbers to show just how little of it gets used. This month, under a new state law, Virginia’s utilities reported theirs. Over the year ending in July, the distribution grid run by Dominion $D Energy, the utility behind the northern Virginia data center boom, was being used at 41% of its capacity on average.

Even in its busiest hours, the system averaged 80%, with some pockets running higher and others lower. In the state’s rural southwest, where demand has been falling, Appalachian Power reported 14% for the year and 54% at peak.

Grids are sized for the few hours a year when everyone runs the air conditioner at once, and building for those few hours is expensive. A data center that pulls full power around the clock raises that peak, and raising the peak means buying another van that mostly sits in the driveway, costing money while it gathers dust.

Researchers argued there might be a cheaper route than building, and it required something we prize in people: Flexibility. Duke $DUK researchers calculated that the existing grid could absorb nearly 100 gigawatts of new demand if the newcomers agreed to cut back its power use for roughly half a percent of the year.

The study caused a stir in energy circles, and Google $GOOGL hired its lead author. The idea came down to a trade. A customer accepts being throttled in the few hours when the local grid is stretched, and in return connects in months instead of years.

The clearest track record of this working belongs to California’s PG&E. EV charging companies, which draw heavily on the grid, saw the wait for power fall to four to eight months from one to three years before its Flex Connect pilot.

In the pilot’s first two years, the power participants asked for was there nine hours out of ten. In the hours it wasn’t, the limits cut into operations less than 1% of the time.

Batteries offered another way to ease the pressure at peak. In 2025, a data center company in Oregon secured a faster hookup from Portland General Electric $GE by agreeing to add a 31-megawatt battery, enough to run about 25,000 homes for two hours, that it could switch to if needed.

Others flexed the computing itself. Google started by putting off work that could wait whenever grids in Belgium and Taiwan ran tight. In 2024, it dialed back machine learning jobs three times when the grid around Omaha was under strain. This year it promised five utilities it would hold back as much as a gigawatt of data center demand when the grid was stretched.

Startups are selling software that slow, pause or relocate AI jobs when the grid is tight. There were plenty of ways to do it, and more kept coming.

Flexibility is already becoming required in some regions. A 2025 Texas law requires new large data centers to install equipment that lets the grid operator cut their power remotely in emergencies. Late last year, FERC, the federal agency that regulates the grid, directed the operator PJM to create interruptible transmission options for data centers built next to power plants. The Southwest Power Pool, which runs the grid across much of the Great Plains, made a similar move.

Of course, waiting is expensive, and data center queues in popular markets can run for years. Take an Nvidia $NVDA chip that costs $30,000 and is depreciated over three to five years, or $6,000 to $10,000 a year. Picture 100,000 of them in one cluster, waiting for power the way Microsoft $MSFT’s chief executive, Satya Nadella, said some of his company’s chips were last year. A year of waiting represents $600 million to $1 billion in potential computing value that goes unused.

The trade cost something on the other side too, although it was much less. For companies racing each other to build more powerful AI, even a few curtailed hours counted. If those 100,000 chips sat idle for the equivalent of 44 hours a year, the lost computing time would be worth $3 million to $5 million.

Flexibility will not fully replace the building the aging grid needs, because old equipment has to go regardless. But utilities planned to spend at least $1.4 trillion through 2030, with data centers and load growth among their top reasons, and a large transformer could take more than two years to arrive. Flexibility could shrink that bill, and that wait.

His Bow Exploded When He Tried to Arrow His Target Buck — But He Still Got the Deer

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 4, 2026 10:00 AM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

Oklahoma bowhunter Max Wynn was sitting in a ground blind with his wife Hartley, not far from a big river bottom in central Oklahoma the evening of October 17. Eventually, he spotted a nice 8-point buck (which was his target deer) and it walked to within just 10 yards of his blind.

“I wanted to take a close [shot at the] buck with my recurve bow, but as I started to draw it, the buck spooked a bit and moved out to 25 yards,” the 24-year-old archer tells Outdoor Life. “I set the recurve bow down, picked up my 75-pound draw weight compound bow, drew it, and released an arrow.

“When I did, it sounded like a cannon went off inside my blind. I set the bow down and saw the string serving was split, the bow string was slack, and both bow cams were bent.”

The arrow that Wynn loosed only flew about 8 yards and hit the dirt. The lighted nock  flew farther than the arrow that it was on.

The bow essentially exploded in his hands, bruising his wrist. Otherwise he was unscathed, though he said he was lucky the broken bow limbs didn’t hit his face.

“The bow limb could have hit the top of my ground blind when I shot my arrow, and that may have caused the bow to explode like it did,” says Wynn, a compression therapist and waterfowl guide for Tine Flies Outfitters. “I was done for the evening and immediately went to Cabela’s and bought a Bear Archery compound bow setup.”

Wynn rigged his Bear bow and sighted it in on targets at home in the truck headlights. The next morning he was back in his ground blind, and shortly a doe came into range at 28 yards. He took the deer with a well-placed arrow.

“That gave me confidence in using that Bear bow, and I went back to the same ground blind that evening with my brother-in-law Maddox Allbright,” Wynn says. “He’s 17 and wanted a doe for the youth deer season. But he decided to wait before taking a doe to see if the 8-point buck I was after came back into the area.”

Indeed, the big buck did come back – following another smaller buck.

“He walked in on the same trail where the doe came that morning, but when he got to where I shot her, he skirted the spot and went a little wider from my blind,” Wynn says. “But he offered a 30-yard quartering away shot. It was a good hit, and he only traveled 35 yards before falling.”

They got the 186-pound 8-pointer out of the woods, and Wynn started processing the buck.

Read Next: Do Deer Really ‘Jump the String?’ Here’s What All Bowhunters Should Know

“I was working my knife on the deer near the ribs by its shoulder and felt something sharp,” Wynn says. “I dug around and pulled out a Slick Trick broadhead that was buried in its chest cavity.

“There were no marks on the outside of the buck’s hide from an arrow wound. The tissue all around the broadhead was healed. The deer looked perfectly healthy before I shot it. Just shows how tough whitetails are.”

Wynn says he was lucky in dressing his 8-pointer that he didn’t cut his hands on the broadhead buried in the deer.

“I think I used up a lot of my deer hunting luck during those two days in the ground blind, and then dressing the buck with another broadhead inside,” he says lightheartedly.

Bob McNally has been an outdoor writer since shortly after the earth’s crust cooled. He has written 12 outdoor books, more than 5,000 outdoor magazine stories (including many for Outdoor Life) and more newspaper outdoor columns and features than there are hairs on a grizzly bear.

The Crunchbase Tech Layoffs Tracker

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The past week has seen at least a dozen companies from both overseas and the U.S. reporting tech layoffs.

Leading by the count of workers affected is New South Wales, Australia-based Canva, an online design and visual communications platform. According to an update posted on its website, the company is reducing its global workforce by 22%, or 1,271 people. With workers based in Austin and San Francisco, it is unclear how many of those layoffs will affect U.S. employees.

Amazon rejoins the tracker this week with a pair of reported layoffs. The first involves 250 workers in its devices and services division, a cutback that took place last week, and another 95 employees in positions including software engineers, product managers and operations employees. According to a report in TheStreet, the affected workers are scheduled to be laid off between Dec. 7 and Jan. 25.

According to a report in the Sacramento Business Journal, Santa Clara, California-based Intel has laid off 67 workers from its Folsom, California, location.

In the lone shutdown reported this week, Ollie AI, a subscription-based AI assistant, will officially close the app on Nov. 6. According to a report in TechCrunch, Bill Lennon, the San Diego-based company’s co-founder and CEO, cited the pressures of the burgeoning AI industry, saying “We got close, but ultimately couldn’t keep up.”

The following companies were added to the tracker this week:

Layoffs during the weeks ended Nov. 4, 2026: At least 2,982 U.S. tech sector employees were laid off or scheduled for layoffs, per a Crunchbase News tally.

In 2025: Around 127,000 workers were let go from U.S.-based tech companies according to our tally.

In 2024: At least 95,667 workers at U.S.-based tech companies lost their jobs in 2024, according to a Crunchbase News tally.

In 2023: More than 191,000 workers in U.S.-based tech companies (or tech companies with a large U.S. workforce) were laid off in mass job cuts.

In 2022: More than 93,000 jobs were slashed from public and private tech companies in the U.S.

This tracker includes layoffs conducted by U.S.-based companies or those with a strong U.S. presence and is updated at least bi-weekly. We’ve included both startups and publicly traded, tech-heavy companies. We’ve also included companies based elsewhere that have a sizable team in the United States, such as Klarna, even when it’s unclear how much of the U.S. workforce has been affected by layoffs.

Layoff and workforce figures are best estimates based on reporting. We source the layoffs from media reports, our own reporting, social media posts and layoffs.fyi, a crowdsourced database of tech layoffs.

We recently updated our layoffs tracker to reflect the most recent round of layoffs each company has conducted. This allows us to quickly and more accurately track layoff trends, which is why you might notice some changes in our most recent numbers.

If an employee headcount cannot be confirmed to our standards, we note it as “unclear.”

A layoff can be either a permanent termination of someone’s employment — usually for cost-saving reasons — or a temporary one because there’s not enough work to justify a full workforce. Tech company layoffs generally fall into the permanent category.

A mass layoff is when a significant number of a company’s employees are cut in a short period of time, often as a result of economic conditions.

Tech layoffs started to surge in 2022 and continued in 2023 and 2024. Companies have given various reasons for conducting layoffs.

Some companies — especially those in the e-commerce sector — nearly doubled their employee headcount to meet consumer demand during the COVID-19 pandemic’s stay-at-home mandates, and later found that they were overstaffed as daily life returned to normal.

Large tech employers such as Salesforce and Google parent Alphabet noted that their post-pandemic layoffs followed several years of rapid hiring fueled by fast growth — between 2019 and 2022, some companies nearly doubled their employee headcount. Some large tech companies also cited slowing sales and fears of a recession as reasons for downsizing.

Venture-backed startups, meanwhile, also cut jobs as a way to cut costs and preserve their cash reserves, as venture funding fell significantly after the peak in 2021. Some startups that ran out of cash and couldn’t raise new funding found themselves filing for bankruptcy or shutting down.

Intel Corp. laid off the largest number of people among U.S. tech employers in 2024, by our count. The semiconductor giant laid off more than 15,000 employees last year.

It was followed closely by electric-car maker Tesla, which cut more than 14,000 roles, and networking company Cisco, with more than 10,000 total roles cut.

In 2023, Amazon layoffs led the numbers with 16,000 roles cut. Layoffs at Alphabet, the parent company of Google, totaled about 12,000, and Microsoft’s layoffs totaled about 10,000 workers in 2023, as did Facebook parent Meta’s layoffs.

Many venture-backed tech startups have also done layoffs as venture capital investment has fallen sharply since the peak in 2021, and falling startup valuations factor into their decisions to conduct layoffs.

Yes, more layoffs are likely coming. While there are signs that the volume of layoffs is tapering, experts we talked to expect job cuts in the tech sector to continue for the foreseeable future as large tech companies and startups continue to battle economic headwinds.

Seed and early-stage startups in particular may continue to conduct layoffs in an attempt to extend their cash runways in a difficult venture funding environment.

Tech layoffs noticeably increased at the start of 2022, ramped up in 2023, waned somewhat in 2024 and have continued in 2025.

Signs that may indicate a company is more likely to conduct layoffs include:

Layoffs declined in 2024 compared to the previous year, but are expected to continue into 2025, although likely not at the level seen in 2023. Startups that need to preserve cash to extend their runways are more likely to conduct layoffs.

Tech layoffs will likely slow when venture capital funding increases and the startup economy stabilizes, as well as when market conditions improve and more startups gain liquidity via IPOs.

Startups that raised capital during the venture funding heyday at inflated valuations in 2021 are more likely to need to conduct layoffs if they’re not able to sustain their previous valuations or growth projections.

Tech layoffs started surging in the 2022 market correction, with an estimated 93,000 U.S. tech workers laid off that year. That figure more than doubled in 2023, with around 200,000 U.S. tech employees laid off, according to our Tech Layoffs Tracker. Layoffs abated again in 2024, with around 95,000 reported tech layoffs.

Keep in mind, many companies don’t report detailed layoffs figures, and some companies continue hiring after cuts for positions deemed more beneficial to the business.

No, according to experts in the sector, who say a sale is unlikely to solve a tech company’s cash-flow issues.

This is especially true as fewer startup M&A deals are happening in the current downturn.

Tech layoffs have hit across departments at many companies.

Many layoffs from the large tech giants were software engineers. Startups tend to be more likely to retain engineers in favor of doing layoffs in their talent and recruiting, marketing and other departments.

Google cut roles in its sales, recruiting, product and engineering teams. Amazon layoffs included jobs in its AWS cloud unit, at its social video platform Twitch, and in its advertising department. Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg said the company’s recruiting department would be the first to see job cuts.

Follow all of our tech layoffs news here and track which companies are cutting jobs with the layoffs tracker above.

Many tech companies continue to hire for open roles, despite layoffs in the sector. Find out more about Crunchbase’s Actively Hiring filter and how you can find companies with multiple open roles.

Guarin: "Via dall'Inter perché ingestibile. L'alcol un'ossessione. I dirigenti dissero..."

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Guarin: "Via dall'Inter perché ingestibile. L'alcol un'ossessione. I dirigenti dissero..."

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

"Ho toccato il fondo, adesso sto risalendo. Mi sento meglio, inseguo la felicità. Racconto la mia esperienza ai più giovani così che possa aiutarli a non sbagliare". Esordisce così Fredy Guarin in un'intervista alla Gazzetta dello Sport. L'ex centrocampista dell'Inter racconta il suo calvario coinciso con i suoi anni a Milano.

"L’Inter mi è rimasta nel cuore, il mio sogno era tornare a San Siro e l’anno scorso l’ho realizzato. Anche i tifosi sono speciali. Dispiace non aver reso al massimo in nerazzurro. Sono sempre stato fedele, anche quando stavo per passare alla Juventus. Io non volevo andare via".

“Eh, sa, la vita è una sliding doors continua. So che Antonio mi voleva. Io alla fine sono rimasto all’Inter e va bene così. Avevo anche la possibilità di andare al Galatasaray da Mancini. Poi, in ogni caso, sono triste per come è andata a finire. Ho fallito tutti gli obiettivi, calcistici e personali. Ero completamente preso dall'alcol”.

“Eccome. Io già quando giocavo bevevo e per me non era un discorso di socialità, ma una vera e propria ossessione. Bevevo da solo, ovunque: a casa, al ristorante, dopo gli allenamenti e dopo le partite. Il calcio per me era una valvola di sfogo, solamente in campo riuscivo a staccare. Poi tornavo a casa e mi ubriacavo da solo. Ero entrato in un loop da cui non riuscivo a uscire”.

“I dirigenti dissero ai miei procuratori di portarmi lontano da Milano e dall’Italia: ero diventato ingestibile. Purtroppo, però, anche quando sono andato via non è cambiato niente. Ho toccato il fondo. Ho una frase tatuata, l'ho scritta io stesso: 'Ho paura della morte e del carcere' e, senza saperlo, vivevo in un carcere da condannato a morte. Ho rischiato più volte di togliermi la vita, ero fuori controllo. La depressione e l’alcol sono due mostri che ti trascinano all’inferno”.

Earwax’s surprisingly slippery but sticky properties deters insects

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

Earwax is anything but waxy and may help protect the ear against insect intrusions

This fire ant is struggling to walk on a clump of earwax, a substance that experts think may exist in part to help keep our ears free of insects.

There are few things more annoying than having a bug fly into your ear. But the body is prepared to fend off these insect invasions, says David Hu, a bioengineer at Georgia Tech.

The goop inside our ears may act as a natural insect repellent, Hu’s team reports 28 October in Soft Matter. What we call earwax is not really waxy at all. Instead, the brownish substance absorbs water, a property that might help it regulate moisture in the ear and keep bugs out, the new analysis shows. “[This] familiar biological material may represent a sophisticated physical defense against small arthropods,” says Stanislav Gorb, a functional morphologist at the University of Kiel in Germany, who was not involved with the work.

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

Researchers have not studied earwax as much as other body fluids, such as snot, spit or even pee. “It’s a big mystery why we even have [it] in our ears,” Hu says. It can frankly seem like a nuisance. Earwax can build up, clogging earbuds and hearing aids and interfering with hearing, so understanding this substance better may help resolve those problems.

Hu and colleagues analyzed the mechanical and chemical properties of 36 earwax samples from pigs, sheep, dogs, cows, rabbits and humans. They also observed how fire ants (Solenopsis invicta) reacted to both natural and synthetic earwax.

Even though ants seem able to scale kitchen cabinets with ease to invade countertops, they flounder when they attempt to climb earwax. Hu thinks the earwax sucks moisture from the ants’ feet that helps them grip. But earwax also sticks to ants’ bodies, causing the insects to start grooming incessantly trying to get rid of it. “It’s both sticky and slippery,” Hu says.

The chemical analyses provide some clues about the origins of these properties. Dead skin cells make up 60 percent of earwax, along with a smattering of oils and moisture. The earwax contains pores that Hu believes may be shaped like ink bottles to help it absorb twice its weight in water and prevent water from sloshing around in the ear canal. Like snot, earwax has a huge range of consistencies.

Under normal conditions, earwax becomes dry and crumbly in about 10 weeks. As it dries, it’s moved outward by a layer of cells lining the ear canal that act as a cleaning conveyor belt. In this way, the ear maintains just the right thickness of earwax to keep insects at bay, Hu says. “It’s most potent when it’s thin,” he explains. “Too much, and the ants can use their tiny claws to grab hold and climb in.”

Hu admits there’s no practical way to turn earwax into a commercial insect repellent. Each person has a volume of earwax about equivalent to that of a grain of rice in each ear at any given time, says Hu, so even if you add up all the earwax on Earth, “it’s a pretty rare substance.” But, he adds, companies “are trying to come up with biodegradable and biofriendly ways to deal with insects.” And, says Gorb, perhaps earwax “could inspire a new generation of physical, nontoxic pest control surfaces,” that could provide coatings for protecting stored food, agricultural products and technology.

Questions or comments on this article? E-mail us at feedback@sciencenews.org | Reprints FAQ

D. Ancalle et al. Earwax as pest control. Soft Matter. 2026. https://doi.org/10.1039/d6sm00737f

Liz Pennisi writes about genomics, evolution, microbiology and organismal biology. She has an undergraduate degree in biology from Cornell University and a master's degree in science writing from Boston University.

"Il n’y a personne à qui tu ne plais pas, Gaël" : les messages touchants de Rafael Nadal et Novak Djokovic pour les adieux de Gaël Monfils

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Mardi soir, Gaël Monfils a fait ses adieux au tennis professionnel au Rolex Paris Masters. Pour saluer le départ du joueur français, Rafael Nadal et Novak Djokovic lui ont notamment chacun adressé un message vidéo, diffusé sur les écrans géants du court central.

Organisée le mardi 3 novembre 2026 en session de soirée, cette célébration s'est tenue sur le court central de la Plénitude Arena de Nanterre, après la victoire de l'Américain Ben Shelton face à Cameron Norrie et avant le duel opposant Casper Ruud à Rafael Jódar. Âgé de 40 ans, Gaël Monfils avait officialisé sa retraite le mois dernier.

Diffusé sur les écrans géants lors de cet hommage, le message vidéo de Rafael Nadal a particulièrement marqué les esprits. L'Espagnol s'est remémoré leur jeunesse commune et la longévité exceptionnelle de leurs parcours respectifs sur le circuit. En utilisant délibérément le pronom « nous », le Majorquin a ainsi associé sa propre trajectoire à celle de Gaël Monfils après plus de vingt ans passés à se côtoyer au plus haut niveau. Un clin d'œil appuyé à leur route partagée depuis les tournois juniors.

Rafael Nadal n'était d'ailleurs pas le seul cador à honorer Gaël Monfils lors de cette soirée à la Plénitude Arena de Nanterre. La vidéo projetée sur le court central comprenait également des messages signés Roger Federer et Novak Djokovic. Dans son intervention, le joueur serbe a notamment salué la personnalité du joueur français dans les vestiaires.

Au cours de ses 22 années de carrière sur le circuit ATP, conclues avec un sommet au 6e rang mondial et 13 titres conquis, Gaël Monfils a pu mesurer une dernière fois l'attachement des supporters. Sur le court, il a effectué un tour d'honneur aux côtés de Gilles Moretton, président de la FFT, et de Cédric Pioline, directeur du tournoi, avant d'être rejoint par Jo-Wilfried Tsonga, Richard Gasquet et Gilles Simon. Le Tricolore a ensuite pris la parole pour remercier chaleureusement le public français, ses parents et sa fille Skai, âgée de quatre ans.

Après cet hommage, le Rolex Paris Masters se poursuit ce mercredi 4 novembre 2026 avec les rencontres du deuxième tour, dont le match programmé entre Arthur Fils et Tomáš Macháč.

Juventus extend Elimoghale to 2029 as Carnevali puts youth sector at the centre

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Juventus have tied academy forward Destiny Elimoghale to the club until 30 June 2029, on the day Giovanni Carnevali spoke to shareholders about the youth sector. The 2009-born striker has already been named in a European squad while injuries stretch the first team.

The club announced Elimoghale’s renewal on 3 November. That was the same day chief executive Giovanni Carnevali addressed shareholders at the Allianz Stadium. He called for a single project covering the first team, the Next Gen and the youth sector.

Elimoghale has come through every level of the club’s academy. He has been at Juventus since childhood. This season he began playing professional football in Serie C.

The NEC fixture was the opening matchday of the Europa League league phase. Spalletti named three Next Gen players because the first team was hit by injuries, Tuttosport reported. Pagnucco and Owusu joined Elimoghale. All three were in a European squad for the first time.

Elimoghale did not get on the pitch in the comfortable win. He stayed on the bench as an unused substitute, alongside Vicario, Pinsoglio and Pagnucco.

Back in Serie C, his minutes have been mixed. In a 5-3 home defeat to Albinoleffe on the sixth matchday, he tried two long-range shots. One went just wide. Goalkeeper Baldi saved the other. At Trento on the 11th matchday, he replaced Leone in the 70th minute of a 2-1 defeat. The match came in Serie C Group A. He was among Massimo Brambilla’s unused substitutes in the 1-1 home draw with Pro Vercelli on 2 November. That matchday 12 fixture was the Next Gen’s most recent game.

Carnevali said the club must bring its youth sector back to the level of recent years. He added that Juventus must keep trying to improve it. Securing a home-grown forward for the long term is consistent with that message.

Carnevali confirmed a change to the UEFA squad list as well, with Ekhator replacing Locatelli.

Aggiornamento della lista UEFA: Jeff Ekhator subentra a Manuel Locatelli. pic.twitter.com/I3Mi1W0PSB

— JuventusFC (@juventusfc) November 3, 2026

For Elimoghale, the wider picture is a first team still short of bodies. Coaches have already turned to the Next Gen once in Europe. A new long-term deal means the club has secured one of those options.

The squad remains stretched heading into the next round of European fixtures. McKennie is suffering from muscle fatigue and will miss the Europa League trip to AZ Alkmaar. Alajbegovic and Cabal are expected to be available in the Netherlands. The trip comes with the European squad list already being reshaped.

« Je n'aime pas ce type de résultat » : la mise au point de Luis Enrique après la victoire 6-0 du PSG

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Au terme de la démonstration du Paris Saint-Germain sur le terrain de Villarreal (0-6), Luis Enrique a livré une analyse très sobre en conférence de presse. Après ce large succès, l'entraîneur espagnol a confié sa gêne face à l'ampleur du score, rappelant son respect pour l'adversaire.

Mardi 3 novembre 2026, à l'Estadio de la Cerámica, le Paris Saint-Germain a signé une prestation éclatante lors de la 4e journée de la phase de ligue de la Ligue des champions. La rencontre a basculé d'entrée avec quatre buts parisiens inscrits dans les 17 premières minutes, dont trois réalisations en l'espace de cinq minutes. João Neves a ouvert le score (9e), rapidement imité par Désiré Doué (12e), Senny Mayulu (13e), Fabián Ruiz (17e, 50e) et David Boly (78e). Un récital offensif impressionnant.

Avec ce large succès (0-6), le PSG totalise 12 points en quatre matchs de phase de ligue et égale la série d'invincibilité record d'un club français en Ligue des champions, soit 14 matchs, établie par Lyon entre 2004 et 2006.

Présent devant les journalistes après la victoire 6-0 à Villarreal, Luis Enrique a confié son embarras face à l'ampleur du score : « Je n'aime pas ce type de résultat », soulignant sa peine de voir son adversaire dans une telle détresse. L'entraîneur du PSG a insisté sur le fait que son équipe respecte toujours l'adversaire en jouant avec la même intensité tout le match sans fermer le jeu, citant en exemple la finale de Ligue des champions remportée face à l'Inter Milan.

Au-delà du score, cette soirée a été marquée par des moments forts en émotion. En effet, Luis Enrique a adressé ses condoléances au CD Tenerife et à la famille du joueur Jesús de Miguel après son décès tragique. D'ailleurs, Fabián Ruiz a lui aussi rendu hommage à l'attaquant dès le coup de sifflet final au micro de Canal+.

Malgré cette démonstration européenne, Luis Enrique a tenu à calmer l'euphorie ambiante en rappelant que rien n'était acquis et que l'objectif premier demeurait le top 8. L'entraîneur a notamment prévenu : « nous ne sommes pas encore champions, ni en finale à Madrid ». Pas question de relâcher la pression.

Toutefois, les Parisiens doivent rapidement basculer sur les affaires courantes. Le prochain match du Paris Saint-Germain est programmé le samedi 7 novembre 2026 à 20h45 au Parc des Princes face à l'ESTAC Troyes en Ligue 1.
